## Page 5

Acknowledgements 
 
This manual is dedicated to the many ORION Plus and ORION NanoFab users who have helped support 
the development of this technology.  Your interest in GFIS technology drove its development and your 
continued support drives us to continue to improve the technology to enable you to do your best work.   
In particular, Annalena Wolff, Ph.D., Queensland University of Technology, Institute for Future 
Environments, Central Analytical Research Facility, contributed her document “HIM ORION NanoFab 
Working Instructions” as a starting point for all the improvements you read in this manual.  Dr. Wolff’s 
documentation, combined with countless contributions and support from ZEISS management, 
applications specialists, software engineers and R&D engineers have turned this from an idea into reality. 
 
Bob Newey, ZEISS Technical Writer

## Page 11

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 6 |
1
Overview
1.1
Precise Imaging, Plus Options
The ORION NanoFab uses a beam of helium or neon ions, produced from a 
proprietary gas field ion source (GFIS), which is focused and scanned across the 
sample. The source technology, the sample interaction, and the contrast mechanisms 
are distinctly different from the traditional scanning electron microscope (SEM). The 
helium ion offers high brightness and a small energy spread, and hence allows the 
beam to be focused to small probe sizes. The ion beams can be precisely controlled 
with variable energy, variable beam currents, and with steering to achieve sub-
nanometer beam positioning with sub-microsecond timing. As the beam interacts with 
the sample, the beam penetrates relatively deeply before it diverges and hence there 
is a narrow sample interaction region near the surface. The helium beam generates 
secondary electrons, scattered helium atoms (ions and neutrals), and other detectable 
particles from which images can be generated or analysis can be performed.
Adding the Capella Gallium Focused Ion Beam (Ga FIB) Column, an optional 
component of the ORION NanoFab basic system, adds a host of additional 
applications to the tool. With the Capella column, gallium ions are emitted, 
accelerated, focused, and deflected, adding FIB imaging, milling, and gas-assisted 
etching and deposition (with the GIS option) to the tasks that can now be performed.
Figure 1. The ORION NanoFab Workstation
GFIS Column

## Page 12

| 7 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
1.2
Beyond Imaging
This tool offers a tremendous range of new capabilities, from imaging, to analysis, to 
nanofabrication and to characterization. The high brightness, highly focused ion 
beams have only recently been available, and as such offer many new possibilities for 
usage in a wide range of applications.
In short, the NanoFab serves as the operator’s “eyes and hands” at the nanoscale. As 
“eyes”, the ion beams generate images with incredibly high resolution. Depending on 
the sample and the imaging conditions, the images can reveal information through a 
range of contrast mechanisms that can include topography, compositional differences, 
crystal grains, and voltage contrast.
In addition to imaging, the NanoFab can be used at higher dosages as “hands” in the 
nanoworld; that is, tools to modify and manipulate samples. For example, the ion 
beam’s sputtering process can create precision cuts and drilled holes that are smaller 
than 5 nm. With the addition of gas phase precursors, the ion beam can be used as a 
nanoscale pen, to deposit either conductive or insulating materials with an arbitrary 
pattern and with features as small as 10 nm. Lighter ion beams can also be used to 
expose negative tone (or positive tone) resists to change their properties so that only 
the exposed (or un-exposed) regions persist after the development process. Ion 
beams can also be used to implant dislocations into a material with an otherwise 
orderly atomic structure and correspondingly change the physical properties in the 
prescribed area.  
In short, the NanoFab’s ion beams can serve as versatile tools for manipulating matter 
on the nanometer scale.
1.3
About This Document
This document has been written for two purposes: to provide hands-on information for 
you, the operators of this tool, who may or may not have experience with the Helium 
Ion Microscope (HIM), and to provide broader understanding to those of you who may 
want to understand the ORION NanoFab and the possibilities it presents in the field of 
material science more thoroughly.
Section 1, Overview 
A brief statement that presents the many possibilities inherent in the use of such a 
powerful imaging, characterization, deposition and milling tool.
Section 2, Safety
A presentation of the safety issues that you must be aware of when working with the 
tool.

## Page 13

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 8 |
Section 3, NanoFab Components and Sub-systems 
Information presented in order to give greater understanding of the various elements 
of the ORION NanoFab system, for those who want to understand it better. This 
information is not necessary to the day-to-day operation of the tool.
Section 4, Routine Operations/Tasks
Provides general information about several of the applications and operations that you 
will be involved in as a user: Online Help, Zen Interface Help, Loading and unloading 
samples, Tuning and Operating the Flood Gun and a few other operations.
Section 5, Tool Setup & Maintenance
Provides specific information about a number of tasks that you will be involved in as an 
operator, such as basic system checks, aligning the source, aperture alignments, 
exchanging the large external N2 Dewar, cleaning the chamber, etc.
Section 6, A Work Session
Takes you through a typical working session at the operator’s console, from making 
your pre-checks, through powering up, to recording and saving your images and 
setting the microscope to Standby mode.
Section 7, Basic Troubleshooting
Explains the primary components of the Vacuum Dashboard. Gives you situations and 
solutions on a number of issues that may arise while working with the tool.
Section 8, GFIS Column Tool Panel
A summary of the principle selections in the GFIS Column tool panel in the UI, and an 
explanation for what those selections control.
Section 9, Presets and Probe Tables
A description of each of these features that can be used to make your use of the tool 
simpler by the construction of machine presets, and the construction of probes and 
probe tables to configure your column.
Section 10, Correlative Microscopy
An overview of the Shuttle & Find module for use in correlative microscopy. An 
overview of the module and step by step instructions for using it.
Section 11, Some Imaging Concepts
A brief synopsis with supporting graphics of several of the concepts that you will be 
dealing with when imaging with the HIM.

2
Safety
Follow the safety instructions given in this operator’s manual. This is essential to 
prevent damage and to protect yourself and other people against accidents and 
unsafe practices. Do not deviate from the instructions provided in this manual.
This section summarizes possible hazards and recommended safety procedures and 
includes the following topics:
•
Safety Labels
•
Safety Equipment
•
Material Safety Data Sheets
2.1
Safety Labels
The following table identifies and describes safety labels on the NanoFab that 
operators should be aware of.
Label
Description
Position
Safety Label
Indicates potential hazards.
360735-0000-029
Attached to the fill hose.
Magnetic Fields Label / Pacemaker Hazard
Static magnetic field may reach 20 mT at 
chamber surface
360735-0000-005
Attached to:
• Ion pump
• Pirani gauge
Burn Hazard Label
Severe skin burns can result from prolonged 
contact.

within the gas box. There are no customer

qualified Zeiss personnel should service the gas 
box. The gas box does not contain any toxic 
gases.
360735-0000-007
Attached to the lower part of the 
gas box at the back of the

2.2
Safety Equipment
This section describes the following NanoFab safety equipment:
•
Protective Cover Panels
•
Emergency Machine Off (EMO) Shutoff
•
Main Shutoff Valves
The customer is responsible for installing main shut-off valves and for providing 
instructions for operating the main shut-off valves.
2.2.1
Protective Cover Panels
The plinth is secured with protective cover panels (see Figure 2).
Note:
The cover panels should only be removed by trained Zeiss service 
engineers.
Figure 2. Protective Cover Panels
Danger
Electrocution hazard: 
High-voltage electrical currents present in the tool can be fatal.

360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
2.2.2
Emergency Machine Off (EMO) Shutoff
The Emergency Machine Off (EMO) buttons are to be used only in an emergency that 
requires an immediate shutdown of all electrical power to the microscope. Pressing 
any of the EMO buttons turns off the output from the UPS, removing all voltages from 
the microscope.
The microscope typically remains powered-up at all times; the chamber is under 
vacuum, the ion source is at cryogenic temperature, and all of the electronics are 
operating. Do not press an EMO button unless there is an actual emergency.
Powering-off the microscope by pressing an EMO button does not allow the software 
running the system computer to save data and shut down properly, and data may 
therefore be lost. Damage may also occur to the turbo pumps.
The NanoFab comes equipped with (2) EMO buttons as shown in Figure 3. 
•
Front panel of the chase rack
•
Front of the microscope
Note:
Other EMO buttons may be added to the microscope.
Figure 3. EMO Buttons
When an EMO button is pressed, it latches in the OFF position. To release the EMO 
button from the latched position, turn the knob in the clockwise direction. All EMOs 
must be in the ON position, or unlatched, to enable the output from the UPS.
Danger
Electrocution hazard: 
High-voltage electrical currents present in the tool can be fatal.

Main Shut-Off Valves
The customer is responsible for the installation of main shut-off valves at the site of 
installation. The following main shut-off valves are required:
•
Nitrogen supply

The main shut-off valves must be easily accessible. They must close off the 
connections to the corresponding media when needed. The main shut-off valves 
must be lockable in their OFF position in order to prevent accidental re-activation.
As the customer is responsible for installing the main shut-off valves, the customer 
should also provide instructions on how to operate the main shut-off valves 
properly.

3.1
GFIS Column
The term “GFIS Column” has two different meanings. In its larger sense, it refers to the 
structure that includes the gun and the column components and housing down to the 
process chamber. In a narrower sense, it refers to the specific portion of the column 
housing and components that sits between the gun and the process chamber, 
containing the parts from lens 1 down through lens 2, which can be lifted out in one 
piece for maintenance. We describe the individual components of the GFIS column in 
its larger sense here.
3.1.1
GFIS Column Elements
GFIS Gun
The gun sits at the top of the GFIS column. It includes the gun head, the source and 
the extractor. It is the portion of the column that tilts back when opening the top of the 
column for source maintenance. The gun is kept at cryogenic temperature. The area is 
pumped by the gun turbo. The gun pressure gauge monitors the pressure in the gun 
area, which should be in the 5E-10 Torr range. 
The rough pump is used for backing the gun turbo pump. Between the rough pump 
and the turbo is the gun safety valve. It is typically open except when the rough pump 
is not in running mode. The gun foreline gauge monitors the pressure in the foreline 
between the turbo and the rough pump. This pressure should be in the 1E-4 Torr 
range.
Figure 5. The GFIS Gun
Gas line
Source
Gun head

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 22](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-022.webp)

*Page 22 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 23

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 18 |
Source
The source is mounted at the top of the column, in the gun assembly. The source 
consists of an insulating base, a filament, and an emitter as shown in Figure 6. The 
insulating base is a ceramic disc in which two electrical connector pins are 
embedded. The filament is high-resistance wire, formed into a V-shape and welded 
between the two pins. One end of the emitter, which is a short length of metal wire, 
is welded to the filament. The other end of the emitter is then sharpened to a point 
by an electro-chemical etching process.
Figure 6. Source Components
The emitter occasionally requires reheating in order to reshape the tip, using the 
Forming the Source routine in the Zen UI. As a final step, the source must be 
sharpened to a point consisting of three atoms at the tip, using the Select a Trimer 
routine. One of the three atoms is then selected for the beam. 
Figure 7. Atom Selection for Beam
A positive voltage (accelerator voltage) is applied to the source and a negative voltage 
(extractor voltage) is applied to the extractor, creating an extraction field between the 
two electrodes. The magnitude of the electric field is enough to ionize (separate an 
electron from an atom) gas atoms that approach the tip. The volume around the 
source is cryogenically cooled, which slows down the motion of the atoms, causing 
them to remain in the ionization area longer, thus increasing the likelihood of 
ionization. Ionized atoms, being positively charged due to the missing electron, are 
repelled by the positive voltage of the source, creating the ion beam that is directed 
down the column and scanned across the sample.
Glass Disc
Emitter
 Filament
Connector Pins

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 23](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-023.webp)

*Page 23 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 24

| 19 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
The beam from the source can be generated with either helium or, if supplied, neon. 
The choice of gas depends on the customer’s application. The gas selection is made 
by you through the UI.
The source is a consumable item.The life time of a source depends on several factors, 
and is generally between 6 months and 2 years. A source replacement kit 360100-
0000-700 is available and can be ordered through your service organization. 
The high voltage applied to the source is generated by the accelerator power supply 
(PS). The power supply typically operates in the range of 25 kV to 35 kV. The 
accelerator PS feedthru is near the top of the column housing. High voltage should 
always be applied to the source even when the source is in the stand-by mode to keep 
impurities from attaching to the tip.
3.1.2
Extractor 
The extractor electrode is a circular plate that mounts just below the source. The 
extractor power supply is connected to the extractor plate (extractor electrode). The 
ion beam passes through a hole in the middle of the extractor plate. The extractor 
voltage, along with the acceleration voltage create the extraction field that ionizes the 
gas atoms. The extraction field is typically between -25 kV and -35 kV.
The feedthru for the extractor PS is near the top of the column housing, above the 
aperture drive assembly.
The extraction field is kept on even when the GFIS column is in standby mode. This 
helps prevent impurities from attaching to the tip.
GFIS Column Ion Beam
The imaging gas (helium or neon) is ionized at the top of the GFIS column, in the gun 
section, creating the ion beam. The beam passes through lens 1, which condenses the 
beam before it passes through the beam-limiting apertures below. The quadrupole tilts 
and shifts the ion beam to line it up directly with the center of lens 2. After passing 
through the selected beam-limiting aperture and the beam blanker, the beam passes 
through the octopoles, which deflect the ion beam in a raster-like motion across the 
sample.
The action of the ion beam striking the sample causes secondary electrons to be 
dislodged from the sample. The secondary electrons are collected by the ET detector. 
The detector converts the secondary electrons into an electrical signal proportional to 
the number of secondary electrons collected from each point of the scan. The 
electrical signal is converted into an image that is displayed within the user interface.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 24](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-024.webp)

*Page 24 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 25

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 20 |
Figure 8. The GFIS Column Ion Beam
The following sections describe the main components of the GFIS column as shown in 
Figure 8.
Lens 1
Lens 1, which is sometimes referred to as the condenser lens, is a three-element, 
electrostatic lens (called an Einzel lens) that forces the ions in the beam to converge 
somewhere above the beam limiting apertures. Any charged-particle beam tends to 
diverge after leaving its source and lens 1 corrects for this divergence and focuses the 
beam towards a point further down the column.
Each element is basically circular with a hole in the middle to allow the beam to pass. 
The uppermost element is electrically connected to the extractor and therefore is at the 
same potential. The lowest element is grounded. The middle element is connected to 
the lens 1 power supply, which has a range of 0 to 40 kV. 
The higher the voltage on this element, the stronger the focus force on the beam, 
causing the focal point, also called the crossover point, to move further up the column.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 25](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-025.webp)

## Page 26

| 21 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Quadrupoles
The main purpose of the quadrupole assembly is to tilt and shift the ion beam so that it 
lines up directly with the center of lens 2. Two quadrupole deflectors, referred to as the 
upper quadrupole and the lower quadrupole, are incorporated in the column 
immediately below lens 1. In Normal Imaging Mode, the quadrupoles are used for 
beam alignment (mid-column tilt and mid-column shift). In SFIM mode, the 
quadrupoles are used to scan the beam.
Figure 9. Beam Tilt & Shift with Quadrupoles
Column Isolation Valve
The column isolation valve (CIV) is a pneumatically operated valve, mounted on the 
front of the column housing, that is opened to allow the ion beam to pass when 
imaging. It is closed to isolate the gun/column vacuum section from the chamber 
vacuum section. The chamber can then be vented for service while keeping the gun/
column sections under vacuum. The valve is closed when the GFIS column is in 
standby mode.
Beam Limiting Apertures
For better imaging, the ion beam must be much narrower than the stream of ions that 
the source produces. Therefore, a beam limiting aperture is used to block all but the 
central part of the ion beam. Through the UI, you can select any one of 14 available 
limiting apertures. Positioning of the apertures is done by the aperture drive assembly, 
located just below the Dewar. The aperture assembly controls the motion of the 
aperture bar, allowing any of 14 apertures to be positioned in the ion beam path. It can 
also pull the aperture bar completely out of the path of the ion beam, allowing the 
beam to pass through what is referred to as “big hole”. The term refers to a position 
where there is no beam-limiting aperture in the beam path.
Every NanoFab is shipped with one of two standard aperture sets. A base system, 
which uses only helium as an imaging gas, is currently shipped with 12 gold foil 
apertures and 2 moly (molybdenum) apertures (Figure 10).

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 26](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-026.webp)

*Page 26 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 27

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 22 |
Figure 10. Base Machine Aperture Set
The decision as to which aperture to use depends on the specific application. Consult 
with your Tool Owner for further information.
A NanoFab that uses both helium and neon as imaging gases is shipped with an 
aperture set containing 8 moly apertures and 6 gold foil apertures (Figure 11).
Figure 11. Aperture Set for Helium and Neon
The six gold foil apertures, positions 2-7, are used for imaging with helium. At high 
magnification and high resolution, the thinner gold apertures give better images. The 
eight moly apertures, positions 1 and 8-14, are used for neon imaging, as gold foil 
apertures are easily damaged with neon. 
Beam Blanking Assembly
The beam blanking plates are mounted on opposite sides of the beam path and 
electrically isolated from each other. During imaging, both plates are grounded, 
allowing the beam to pass between them unaffected. From there the beam passes 
down the optical axis and through the hole in the middle of the beam blanking aperture 
(Figure 12). 
When not imaging, it is best to not allow the beam to continue down the column. This 
is called blanking the beam. Blanking the beam is accomplished by applying an equal, 
but opposite polarity voltage to each of the blanking plates. The field created between 
the two plates deflects the beam away from the optical axis. Once deflected, instead of 
passing through the beam blanking aperture, it strikes the inside of the beam Faraday 
cylinder and is measured.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 27](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-027.webp)

*Page 27 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 28

| 23 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Figure 12. Beam Blanking 
Faraday Cup and Beam Blanking Aperture
The Faraday cup is comprised of the Faraday cylinder and the beam blanking aperture 
attached to the bottom. When the beam is blanked it strikes the inside of the Faraday 
cup, allowing the beam current to be measured by circuitry in the GFIS support 
module.
The beam blanking aperture is a flat plate with a small hole in the middle and attaches 
to the bottom of the Faraday cylinder. When the ion beam is unblanked it passes 
through the aperture. When the ion beam is blanked, all the ions in the beam are 
captured within the Faraday cup.
Figure 13. The Faraday Cup

Octopoles
An octopole deflector (referred to as an 
octopole) works like a quadrupole deflector 
but with eight radial deflectors, which allows 
for finer control. The GFIS column has two 
octopoles, mounted one above the other, 
referred to as the upper and lower octopoles. 
The octopoles work together to deflect the ion 
beam in a scanning pattern across the 
sample. A double-deflection scheme is used 
which keeps the beam on the center of lens 2 
while scanning. The signals that drive both 
octopoles come from the GFIS optics module.
Figure 14. Octopole Beam Deflection
Lens 2
Like lens 1, lens 2 is a 3-element (Einzel) lens that focuses the ion beam on the 
sample. Each element is basically circular with a hole in the middle to allow the beam 
to pass. The upper and lower elements are grounded. The middle element is 
connected to the lens 2 power supply. The power supply is a positive supply and has a 
range of 0 to 40 kV. The higher the voltage on this element, the stronger the focus 
force on the beam, causing the focal point (working distance) to move closer to the 
column.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 29](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-029.webp)

*Page 29 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
3.2
Process Chamber
The process chamber sits at the base of the tool, below the column. All processing 
takes place inside the process chamber. Samples are placed in the chamber via an air 
lock, which is mounted on the chamber (the back side of the image in Figure 15).
3.2.1
Process Chamber Elements
Cradle & Tilt Drive Assembly
Inside the process chamber is the cradle, which provides motion on one axis, referred 
to as tilt. The tilt axis has a range of travel of -5 degrees to +54 degrees. At +54 it is 
normal to the Capella (Ga FIB) column option, which would be mounted above the 
chamber door. Tilt is accomplished by tilting the cradle, which is driven by the tilt drive 
assembly.
Figure 15. Process Chamber
Stage
Mounted in the cradle is a 4-axis stage. The stage allows four axes of movement; X, Y, 
Z, and Rotate. As an operator, consider that the front of the machine is on the air lock 
side, as that perspective is what you get in your ion beam images.The X-axis therefore 
moves in the left and right direction in your images, and the Y-axis appears as moving 
in the front and back direction, using the air lock as your orientation point. Both the X 
and Y axes have a travel range of 50 mm. The Z-axis is the up-and-down direction, 
with a travel range of 8 mm. The rotate axis has a range of 0 to 360 degrees. 
Sample Holder
The sample holder sits on the sample holder mounting platform on top of the stage 
when it has been inserted into the chamber through the air lock.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 30](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-030.webp)

## Page 31

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 26 |
Camera
The chamber camera is mounted on the back of the process chamber, shown on Figure 
15. This allows you to view the stage and holder as they are moving, to avoid the 
sample hitting the bottom of the column, the ET detector grid, or anything else in the 
chamber.
Also in the process chamber is the detector (not shown) that collects the secondary 
electrons emitted from the sample as it is struck by the ion beam. The gun, column and 
process chamber together compose the process module.
ET Detector
The Everhart-Thornley detector (ET detector) is mounted towards the left on the 
process chamber (not shown in Figure 15). It’s purpose is to collect the secondary 
electrons that are dislodged from the sample when struck by the ion beam, for basic 
and high-resolution imaging, giving information on the topography of the sample, and 
to some degree its composition. Within the ET detector, the secondary electrons are 
converted to an electrical signal proportional to the number of electrons collected.
Figure 16. ET Detector
On the end of the ET detector, inside the process chamber, is a small wire-mesh cage 
called the collector grid. Under normal operation, the grid is at a potential of +500 volts. 
The secondary electrons that are dislodged from the sample are attracted to the 
positive potential on the ET detector grid. Just behind the grid is the scintillator, a disk-
shaped substrate, that is positively biased to attract and accelerate the electrons 
drawn in by the grid. Electrons striking the scintillator cause photons to be emitted 
from the other side of the scintillator. The silica glass light pipe carries photons 
produced by the scintillator out of the vacuum chamber and provides electrical 
isolation between the internal and external components of the detector.
Behind the light pipe is the photomultiplier tube (PMT), which converts the photons to 
an electrical signal and multiplies it many order of magnitude. The gain through the 
PMT is controlled by the voltage applied across it. This voltage is controlled by the 
contrast setting. The output from the PMT is fed to the ET detector pre-amp. The 
output of the pre-amp is on the VIDEO connector of the ET detector assembly, which 
is connected to the SAS for image processing.
Collector grid
Scintillator 
(inside)

containing the light pipe

assembly

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 31](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-031.webp)

*Page 31 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 32

| 27 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Flood Gun
The flood gun is mounted on the chamber above the chamber door.
The NanoFab HIM imaging process involves striking the specimen to be imaged with 
a beam of positively-charged helium ions. When these ions hit the specimen, they 
dislodge (negatively-charged) electrons, referred to as secondary electrons (SE). Both 
actions (adding positive ions and removing electrons) cause a non-conducting 
specimen to become positively charged. The more positively charged the specimen 
becomes, the harder it is for the secondary electrons to be dislodged, since they are 
attracted back to the positively-charged sample instead of making their way to the ET 
detector. No image is formed unless secondary electrons can reach the ET detector. 
To overcome the problem of the specimen becoming positively charged, the flood gun 
generates a beam of electrons that is aimed at the specimen to reduce or eliminate the 
positive charge (Figure 17). Elements within the flood gun allow the beam to be emitted 
(or not), focused, and aimed (or deflected), so that the electron beam can be 
concentrated in the same area on the specimen that the ion beam is striking. This 
process of flooding the specimen with an electron beam is called charge 
neutralization. 
Figure 17. Electron Flooding
When imaging, the grid on the ET detector is positive to attract secondary electrons. If 
the flood gun emitted electrons while the ET detector was positive, the electrons from 
the flood gun would completely saturate the ET detector. To prevent this situation from 
occurring, the ion beam and the electron beam from the flood gun are multiplexed so 
that the flood gun is blanked while the ion beam is on, and vice versa (Figure 18). When 
the flood gun is emitting electrons, the ET detector grid is at a -1000 V potential to 
repel flood gun electrons. This prevents the ET detector from getting saturated.
Figure 18. Imaging
Flood gun
ET detector

3.3
Capella Column Option
The Capella, a Gallium Focused Ion Beam (FIB) Column, is an optional component of 
the ORION NanoFab basic system. It is located on the left side of the chamber, as 
viewed from the front. It is typically used in the following applications:
•
FIB Imaging - Image generation and specimen analysis by a focused ion beam 
scanned across the specimen.
•
Milling - Removal of material from the specimen surface by a focused ion beam 
concentrated locally.
•
Gas-assisted etching - Cutting into the material with the assistance of a process 
gas via a Gas Injection System (GIS).
•
Gas-assisted deposition - Deposition of material using a focused ion beam and 
the assistance of a process gas via a GIS.
Figure 19. Capella (Ga FIB) Column (option)
Gallium ions (Ga+) are extracted from a liquid metal ion source, and are used up 
during operation. The source is rated for 30000 µAh.

3.4
Gas Injection System (GIS) Option
The Gas Injection System (GIS) is a chemical delivery system that allows chemically 
reactive gasses to be released within the process chamber in close proximity to where 
the ion beam impacts the sample.
The beam interacts with the various chemical precursors to either deposit materials 
onto the substrate, or etch materials from the substrate.
Figure 20. Gas Injection System (option)
Both electrical and pneumatic control lines connect the GIS mounted on the chamber 
to the electronic controller located in the options rack. The GIS delivers gas into the 
chamber through a retractable needle that is inserted during operation to be in close 
proximity to the sample substrate.
The GIS delivery system can deliver up to 3 different chemical precursors and two 
different inert gasses. The inert gasses can be used as a process gas or a carrier gas 
for the delivery of the precursor.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 34](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-034.webp)

*Page 34 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 35

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 30 |
3.5
NanoPatterning and Visualization Engine (NPVE) Option
The Fibics NanoPatterning and Visualization Engine (NPVE) is an optional component 
of the ORION NanoFab® and allows you to perform intricate and specific patterns in 
both etching and deposition processes. It incorporates a dedicated 16 bit scan 
generator for each NanoFab column and dual signal acquisition hardware supporting 
real-time advanced patterning and visualization. The beam is completely controlled by 
the GUI that allows it to create a range of fully editable shapes including rectangles, 
trapezoids, polygons, lines, polylines, ellipses and spots; then it‘s possible to vector-fill 
these shapes while maintaining full control over dose variation and patterning 
parameters including patterning direction, spacing, dwell, refresh and scan strategy. 
See Section 12.2, Patterning Basics Using NPVE.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 35](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-035.webp)

*Page 35 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

3.6
Vacuum Subsystem
The Vacuum Dashboard is a good place to observe the operation of the vacuum 
system during normal operation but should be used only for observation and not for 
running the system. 
There are three separate vacuum regions within the NanoFab: gun/column, chamber 
and Dewar.
Figure 21. The Vacuum Dashboard
The area above the column isolation valve in the GFIS column is the gun/column 
vacuum region. This region is pumped by the gun turbo pump at the rear of the 
process chamber. It is shown in Figure 22. 
Figure 22. Gun/Column Vacuum Region
On the right side of the Dashboard is the GFIS Column panel, which shows the pumps

3.7
Dewar System
The integrated Dewar uses liquid nitrogen to cool the ion gun to cryogenic 
temperatures (below 100 K), that are required to obtain useful rates of helium 
ionization. The Dewar vessel is a stainless steel vacuum reservoir mounted on the top 
of the chamber module on the right-hand side of the ion column. The Dewar contents 
are pumped down to a pressure of about 2 Torr. This lowers the boiling point of the N2 
to below 60 K. At this temperature the nitrogen turns to a solid. Heat is transferred from 
the ion gun to the bottom of the inner wall of the vessel by a cold finger linkage, 
designed to maximize the conduction of heat to the nitrogen while minimizing the 
transmission of mechanical vibration to the ion gun.
Figure 27. Integrated Dewar System
The integrated Dewar is automatically refilled with liquid nitrogen from an external 
transfer Dewar tank twice a day. See Section 4.8.1, Auto Fill Schedule. The external tank 
is not part of the NanoFab assembly, but is the customer’s responsibility.
Figure 28. Large N2 Dewar Refill Tank

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 40](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-040.webp)

*Page 40 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 41

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 36 |
3.8
Gas Delivery System
The gas delivery system has all the components needed to provide the clean, 
pressure-regulated gases to the GFIS gun. These components include the gas bottles, 
pressure regulators, motorized leak valves, a gas purifier, and an isolation valve.
The gas delivery system is mounted at the back of the microscope. All NanoFabs have 
helium as an imaging gas, in which case there is one set of valves and gauges for that 
line. Neon can be provided as well as an option, in which case you would have two gas 
feed lines, as shown in Figure 29.
Figure 29. Gas Delivery System
The motorized leak valves control and shut off the flow of process gas. They are 
controlled automatically through the computer.
The gas purifier removes contaminants from the process gas.
The pressure regulators reduce the gas bottle pressure to 4 psi, which is reflected in 
the upper of the two gauges on each line. They are not used by the operator. The two 
lower gauges are high pressures gauges; each one measuring the pressure in the 
bottle.
It would be good practice for the operator to periodically inspect the high pressure 
gauges, as gas bottles are typically replaced when the pressure falls below 500 psi.
The electronic pressure gauge at the back of the gas box transmits the gas box 
pressure to the computer, which is reflected in the gas pressure readout in the Gas 
Box panel of the Vacuum Dashboard. If there is any pressure issues occurring in the 
system, you will get a message in the vacuum screen.
Do not attempt any maintenance of the gas box yourself. Call a service representative 
if any issues arise.
Neon bottle
Helium bottle
Motorized leak valve

3.9
Vibration Isolation System
The vibration isolation system protects the NanoFab from outside vibrations that may 
cause movement of the ion beam relative to the sample, resulting in ripples in the 
image.The vibration isolation system consists of upper isolators and lower isolators as 
shown in Figure 30.
Figure 30. Isolation System
Both sets of isolators are fed by the compressed dry air (CDA) system from the 
pneumatic control panel. The vibration isolation system is enabled or disabled with a 
button labeled air table in the Air Lock and Chamber panel of the Vacuum Dashboard 
(see Figure 31). Clicking on the button toggles the state between On (green indicator) 
and Off (red indicator).
Figure 31. Air Lock and Chamber Panel

5/29/18
Knob Panel
The actions controlled by the knob panel are described in this section.
Figure 34. Knob Panel
Panel Lock – This button toggles between panel locked mode and panel unlocked 
mode. When in panel locked mode all other knobs and buttons (does not affect the 
keyboard buttons or the Panel Lock button itself) are disabled. When the panel is not 
locked, all buttons and knobs are enabled.
Stigmator X and Stigmator Y – These knobs are used to correct any astigmatism of 
the beam by varying small DC voltages applied to the upper octopole deflectors.
Magnification – This knob is used to adjust the magnification, or field of view, of the 
image. Turning the knob clockwise gives the effect of zooming in on the image while 
turning the knob counter-clockwise zooms out.
Reduced – This button toggles between full scan view (the entire FOV) and reduced 
scan view. The reduced scan view results in a scan of a smaller portion of the image, 
and thus a faster image refresh time which can make adjusting focus and stigmation 
easier.
Tilt/Shift X and Tilt/Shift Y – These knobs are used to correct for small errors in the 
mechanical alignment of the ion beam. Tilt error is when the beam in not parallel to the 
axis of the column and shift error is when the beam is laterally offset from the axis of 
the column. This is accomplished by applying small DC voltages to the upper and 
lower quadrupoles.
Mode – This button toggles the mode of the Tilt/Shift knobs between the tilt function 
and the shift function.
SFIM/Normal – This button toggles the image mode between SFIM mode and normal 
imaging mode.

## Page 45

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 40 |
Scan Rotate – This control is both a pushbutton and a knob. Pushing the button 
toggles between scan rotate enabled and scan rotate disabled. When scan rotate is 
enabled, turning the knob clockwise or counter-clockwise rotates the scan, and 
therefore the displayed image to rotate in the clockwise or counter-clockwise, 
respectively.
Start/Stop – This button toggles between imaging and not imaging.
Grab – Pressing this button triggers one full scan, at the current imaging parameters. 
Scanning will stop at the completion of one full scan.
Pan X and Pan Y – These knobs shift the scanning position of the beam on the 
sample, and therefore the displayed image, in the X and Y direction respectively.
Brightness – This knob adjusts a DC offset that is applied to each pixel of the image 
during image processing.
Contrast – This knob adjusts the gain through the photomultiplier tube in the ET 
detector.
Focus – This knob controls the lens 2 voltage which determines the focal distance of 
the beam. This knob is adjusted to bring the area being scanned into focus in the 
image.
Scan Speed (plus and minus) – These two buttons give you a quick way to shorten 
the dwell time (increase scan speed) or lengthen the dwell time (decrease scan 
speed).

4.3
Loading and Unloading Samples
4.3.1
Sample Handling
For the best performance of the NanoFab anything loaded into the process chamber 
must be extremely clean. Always wear new, clean gloves when handling any sample, 
sample holder, or anything that is going inside the process chamber. Always wear 
gloves when touching any surface inside the air lock.
All samples loaded into the NanoFab must be vacuum compatible. The NanoFab 
process chamber and ion columns are operated at high vacuum. Any contamination 
can compromise the performance of the NanoFab. Do not load any samples that that 
outgas as this can lead to severe contamination problems within both the process 
chamber and the ion columns.
All samples must be dry and clean. When mounting sample material on an SEM stub, 
make sure the stub is clean. If it can be done without damaging the sample, use a 
nitrogen gun to blow it and the sample holder off. Make sure the sample is securely 
attached.
Use as little material as possible: 
Too much material always poses a 
risk for device contamination and 
might also prevent you from seeing 
the sample features you are 
interested in (excess material can 
block the view). 
Your sample must be securely 
attached to SEM stub: Loose sample 
debris can contaminate the column, 
resulting in system downtimes for 
several weeks and high associated 
costs. 
Maximum sample dimensions: L x 
W x H = 50 mm x 50 mm x 6 mm. 
Be aware of sample height: High 
sample heights poses the risks of 
smashing into the end of lens 2 and 
the ET detector grid when moving the 
stage. See section 4.4.1, Stage Motion 
Risks.
Loose Sample Debris: Samples like 
powders and small particles that are 
not secured to the stub can, especially 
if non-conductive, get up into the 
column.
Figure 56. System Performance- Prior & After Contamination

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 57](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-057.webp)

*Page 57 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

4.3.2
Sample Load Status
The current sample load status is displayed in the menu bar at the top of the UI (Figure 
57).
Figure 57. Sample Load Statuses
There are three sample statuses:
•
Empty - There is no sample holder on the stage. 
•
Working - A sample exchange is in progress
•
Loaded - There is a sample holder on the stage.
There is no sensor on the stage that determines if a sample holder is loaded; the 
displayed status is based on the last actions of the operator. At the end of the 
Exchange routine, the status reported is Empty, with the assumption that you have 
removed the sample holder. The status is reported as Loaded after the Resume 
routine has been completed.
4.3.3
The Air Lock
The purpose of the air lock (also called the load lock) is to allow the movement of a 
sample into or out of the process chamber without having to vent the process 
chamber.
Figure 58. Air Lock
Between the air lock and the chamber is the gate valve, which provides a vacuum seal 
between the air lock and the process chamber. When the gate valve is closed, the air 
lock can be vented without affecting the process chamber vacuum. When the air lock 
is pumped, the gate valve can be opened to allow the transfer of the sample holder 
between the air lock and the process chamber.
Air lock door
Viewing window
Transfer rod 
Air lock door handle

Note:
When installing or removing the transfer rod, the fixing knob on the end of 
it (Figure 58) has a tight fit due to the knob o-ring. You can damage the 
alignment pins on the fixing knob if you put a wrench to it and turn it too 
far, or in the wrong direction.
When installing the transfer rod, align the pins on the knob with the pin 
slots in the air lock port (if they don’t align, turn the knob 180 degrees). 
Slide the pins into the slots and turn the knob clockwise approximately 5 
degrees. When removing the transfer rod, grasp the fixing knob firmly with 
both hands and turn the knob approximately 5 degrees counter-clockwise 
and slide it out. Tools should not be needed.
Figure 59. Inserting and Removing Transfer Rod Without Damage

The UI version of the air lock control panel, the Sample Management tool panel, has 
more features than the control panel on the side of the air lock. 
The buttons and indicators in 
the Sample Management 
tool panel that match those 
on the air lock control panel 
perform the same functions. 
Each of the controls and

below. The sequence in 
which to use these is 
described in the sections on

Figure 62. Sample Management Tool Panel
Exchange
Use the Exchange button to move the stage into the load position, or stage-ready 
position. The load position is where all the axes of the stage are set so the sample 
holder can be transferred between the air lock and the process chamber using the 
transfer rod.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 60](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-060.webp)

## Page 61

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 56 |
If there is no sample holder on the stage, the exchange routine ends after positioning 
the stage to the load position. Otherwise, the air lock is pumped and the air lock gate 
valve opens. The illumination LEDs also turn on.
Resume
Use the Resume button after placing the sample holder on the stage and fully 
retracting the transfer rod. This causes the air lock gate valve to close and the stage to 
move to a position under the column, in the beam path.
Transfer
Use the Transfer button to pump the air lock and open the gate valve once the stage is 
in the Stage Ready position.
Store
Use the Store button to achieve a state in which the air lock gate valve is closed and 
the air lock rough valve is open in order to pump down the air lock to rough vacuum, at 
which time the rough valve closes. 
Vent
Use the Vent button to vent the air lock. This causes the air lock gate valve to close 
and nitrogen to be pumped into the air lock to vent it. In the vent state the air lock door 
can be opened for loading or unloading of the sample holder into or out of the air lock.
Stage Ready
The Stage Ready indicator lights up when the sample stage is in the load position.
Rod Retracted
The Rod Retracted indicator lights up when the transfer rod is fully retracted. If the 
transfer rod is not fully retracted, any operation involving opening or closing the air lock 
gate valve is inhibited.
Door Open
The Door Open indicator lights up when the air lock door is not fully closed.
Error
The error indicator lights up when the air pressure for the gate valve is too low to 
operate the valve.
Illumination
Use the Illumination button to turn the air lock illumination LEDs on or off. The indicator 
with the Illumination button should indicate whether the illumination is on or off, but 
sometimes goes out of sync and does not correctly indicate the state of the air lock 
illumination. Clicking on the button causes it to indicate the proper state.

Air lock door
The air lock door allows access to the air lock for loading or unloading samples. When 
the air lock is vented, open the door by pulling on the air lock door handle. The door 
must be fully closed in order to pump down the air lock.
Viewing window
Use the viewing window in the air lock door to visually monitor the process of loading 
or unloading the sample on the stage.
Sample transfer rod
Use the sample transfer rod to place the sample holder onto, or removing it from, the 
sample stage. Use the knob on the end of the rod to thread the rod into the sample 
holder, which is threaded on the other end for attachment to the sample holder.

4.4
Stage Control
Note:
All reference to direction assumes that the front of the tool is the aspect of 
the tool containing the air lock. The perspective you see in the ion beam 
images is as if you were standing in front of the air lock.
The stage can be controlled either by using the joysticks or through the UI. The details 
of using either of these methods are described in the following sections. Regardless of 
what controls are used to move the stage, the stage movements are the same, and the 
same precautions must be taken. 
4.4.1
Stage Motion Risks
It is possible to crash the sample holder into either the end of lens 2 or the ET detector 
grid, so care must be taken. Moving the stage in any direction has that risk, depending 
on the sample height, except for moving down on the Z axis. Therefore always use the 
chamber scope image to observe the motion of the stage while moving it to make sure 
the samples stay clear of the GFIS column and the ET detector.
•
Moving the stage to a high Z-value has a high risk of crashing a sample into lens 2 
or the detector grid.
•
Tilting the stage, especially with a high Z value, has a particular risk, in either 
direction.
•
Movement in any direction, except down on the Z-axis, with a tall sample on the 
sample holder, or when moving after viewing a low spot on a sample, has the risk 
of hitting higher locations or higher samples on the sample holder, so you must 
always view the movement through the chamber scope.
Note:
Remember that the chamber scope view is from the camera, which is 
situated 90 degrees to the right of the air lock. 
Figure 81. Chamber Scope view of Sample Holder
Sample 
holder

flood gun and lower) 
Flood gun

+X toward you

5/29/18

| 70 |
4.4.2
Stage Motions
The stage moves in five axes. Each are described below. The front of the NanoFab is 
defined as the side of the tool where the air lock is mounted. This is the orientation in 
which you view your ion beam images. 
Note:
Be aware that in the chamber scope view, the orientation changes 90 
degrees, as the chamber scope camera is mounted 90 degrees to the 
right of the air lock.
X-Axis
The X-axis is defined as the left and right directions when standing in front of the air 
lock. The range of travel of the X-axis is 50 mm. The positive direction is defined as 
towards the right of the chamber (toward the camera). When the X-axis is all the way 
to the left of the chamber the position is defined as 0 mm.
Y-Axis
The Y-axis is defined as the away and back directions when standing in front of the air 
lock. The range of travel of the Y-axis is 50 mm. The positive direction is towards the 
chamber door (to the right in the camera view). When the Y-axis is back toward the air 
lock the position is defined as 0 mm.
Z-Axis
The Z-axis is in the up and down direction, with a travel of 8 mm. The positive direction 
is defined as up (towards the GFIS column). When the axis is all the way down, it is 
defined to be a 0 mm.
Rotate Axis
The rotate axis has a range of travel of 360 degrees. Clockwise is defined as the 
positive direction.
Tilt Axis
The tilt axis has a range of travel of -5 degrees to +54 degrees. Zero degrees is 
defined as when the stage is horizontal (normal to the GFIS beam). The positive 
direction of tilt is when the bottom of the cradle assembly moves toward the air lock. 
This faces the top of the stage towards the chamber door, or the side of the chamber 
on which the Capella (Ga FIB column) option would mount. At a tilt of 54 degrees, the 
top of the stage is normal to the Ga FIB beam.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 75](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-075.webp)

*Page 75 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 76

| 71 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
4.4.3
Joystick Control
Note:
All reference to direction assume that the air lock is at the front of the tool 
with the chamber door at the back of the tool.
Tilt/Z Joystick
If you move the joystick left, the top of the stage tilts toward the air lock (-tilt) up to -5 
degrees.
If you move the joystick right, the top of the stage tilts away from the air lock (+tilt) to a 
maximum of 54 degrees (toward the Capella column location).
If you move the joystick down, the stage platform moves down (-Z)
If you move the joystick up, the stage platform move up (+Z)

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 76](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-076.webp)

*Page 76 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 77

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 72 |
X/Y/R Joystick
If you move the joystick right, the stage platform moves right in relation to the air lock 
(+X), and the image moves right.
If you move the joystick left, the stage platform left in relation to the air lock (-X), and 
the image moves left.
If you move the joystick down, the stage platform moves towards the air lock (-Y), and 
the image moves down.
If you move the joystick up, the stage platform moves away from the air lock (+Y), and 
the image moves up.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 77](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-077.webp)

## Page 80

| 75 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Rotation Control
On the right hand side are the controls for X,Y, and R. 
Figure 84. Rotation Control
On the outside right edge of the control is the rotate control. This control is divided into 
four sections, with a small oval in the middle. There are two ways of controlling 
rotation; one is by dragging the oval, and the other is by entering small and large step 
values in the R, Sm and Lg fields (Figure 83) in the lower left corner of the top section 
of the Stage Positioning tool panel, and then clicking in one of the four sections of the 
rotate control to rotate the stage one step at a time. 
The oval is a virtual joystick. Dragging this oval up causes the rotate axis to move 
continuously for as long as the oval is held away from its center position; up for a 
negative rotation direction, and down for a positive rotation direction. 
The four sections are step controls, meaning that each time the control is clicked, the 
rotate moves a fixed amount. When you hover over a section with the mouse, it 
highlights. The two sections at the upper and lower end of the control rotate in large 
steps and the two inner sections rotate in small steps. The magnitude of the steps are 
determined by the values entered in the fields in the lower left of the window.
Figure 85. Rotation Step Size Values
The X and Y step controls are the two concentric circles, each divided into eight 
sections. The inner circle controls the small steps and the outer circle controls the 
large steps.

4.5
Exchanging the External N2 Supply Tank
The external N2 supply tank is the responsibility of the customer. It is also the 
responsibility of the customer to have all operators trained in the procedures for 
exchanging the external tank as well as all safety precautions that must be observed.
It is recommended that the NanoFab Dewar Control be put into Service Mode while 
the external tank is being exchanged. This prevents any chance of the software 
initiating a Dewar fill while the tank is being exchanged. To put the system into service 
mode, click on the Dewar Fill icon
, which is found along the top of 
the UI. The Dewar Control tool panel opens, with the system in Active Cooling mode. 
See Figure 92.
Figure 92. Dewar Control Tool Panel
Click the Begin Servicing button at the bottom of the pane to disable the Auto Fill 
routine. See Figure 93.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 83](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-083.webp)

*Page 83 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 84

| 79 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Figure 93. Servicing N2 Source
The Dewar Fill icon at the top of the UI flashes yellow on and off, labeled Servicing. 
See figure below.
Figure 94. Dewar Fill Icon - Servicing
After the tank has been exchanged, click the Done Servicing button at the bottom of 
the Dewar Control tool panel. Auto fill is now enabled. It is very important to click this 
Done Servicing button after the tank has been exchanged or Auto Fill remains 
disabled and the gun eventually warms up.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 84](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-084.webp)

4.6
Chamber Cleaning
All vacuum chambers contain volatile hydrocarbon molecules that are introduced into 
the chamber through atmospheric hydrocarbon contamination, lubrication, or 
inadvertent contamination by users. This build-up of hydrocarbon reduces the image 
quality. Hydrocarbon contamination in the chamber causes staining on the sample 
when imaging. It also results in deposits on the sample when milling. Any time these 
symptoms are noticed, it is time to clean the chamber with the plasma cleaner, also 
referred to as the Evactron (brand name). The plasma generated by the Evactron 
chemically reacts with the hydrocarbons in a way that makes them easier to pump out 
of the chamber.
The Evactron consists of two major components:
•
Plasma radical source
•
Controller
The plasma radical source is mounted on the chamber port to the left of the air lock.
Figure 95. Evactron Plasma Radical Source
The controller is mounted in the chase rack. 
Figure 96. Evactron Controller
Evactron
Evactron controller

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 85](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-085.webp)

Performing a Chamber Cleaning
In earlier days, chamber cleaning was performed through the Service Machine 
application. It can now be done directly from the Zen UI. Use it when you have 
indications of hydrocarbon contamination.
Select Maintenance > System > Chamber Cleaning in the UI.
Figure 97. Chamber Cleaning selection
The Chamber Clean screen opens.
You can begin cleaning now, or clean at a pre-determined time by selecting either 
Start Now or Schedule Clean at the bottom of the screen. If you select Start Now, the 
Start Date and Start Time are not relevant. If you schedule a time in the future, you 
must enter the Start Date and Start Time.
Select the Number of hours for the cleaning.
There are 3 selections for Cleaning Intensity in the drop-down list: Mild, Moderate, 
and Heavy. When you select one, the Plasma and Purge values for it display in the 
Actions section at the bottom.
The cleaning process requires the chamber to be at rough vacuum. To accomplish this 
the following sequence executes:
•
The air lock is pumped.
•
The gate valve opens.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 86](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-086.webp)

*Page 86 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 87

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 82 |
•
the chamber turbo pump is turned off. 
It takes a few minutes for the turbo pump to spin down to a stop, during which time the 
status of the turbo pump is reported as braking in the Vacuum Dashboard (red box, 
Figure 98).
Figure 98. Rough Pump Braking
When the turbo has completely stopped, a burst of nitrogen is put into the chamber. 
This causes pressure in the chamber to rise above 10 Torr, at which point the 
ENABLED LED on the front panel of the Evactron controller starts blinking (Figure 99).
Figure 99. ENAPLED On
After a number of minutes, the rough pump gets the chamber down to a pressure in 
the range of 5E-1 Torr (Figure 100). This is the pressure required for the Evactron to 
operate.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 87](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-087.webp)

*Page 87 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 88

| 83 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Figure 100. Chamber Pressure in Range
Once the Evactron pressure reaches the correct level, the RF ON LED on the front of 
the panel of the Evactron controller lights up (Figure 101).
Figure 101. RF On
This means the source in the Evactron is on, which is required to generate the plasma. 
Once the plasma is being generated, the PLASMA ON LED lights up (Figure 101). The 
two yellow LEDs remain lit while the plasma is on and off during the nitrogen purge 
portion of each cleaning cycle. The process repeats until completing the number of 
cycles selected.
When the cleaning process is completed, the software restores the chamber to the 
pumped state.

4.8
Dewar Control
The Dewar Control tool panel in the UI has a few features that are helpful for the 
operator. To open the Dewar Control tool panel, click the Dewar Fill icon in the menu 
bar at the top of the UI. 
Figure 104. Dewar Fill Icon
The Dewar Control tool panel opens (Figure 105).
Figure 105. Dewar Control Tool Panel
4.8.1
Auto Fill Schedule
The top section of the Dewar Control tool panel (Figure 105) is the Auto Fill Schedule 
section. The last time the Dewar was filled is displayed in the Fill was at: field. Below 
that is the time of the next scheduled fill. Below that two text boxes display the 
scheduled times for the two daily auto fills. The fill times cannot be entered directly into 
these text boxes. To change the scheduled auto fill times, click on the Adjust 
Schedule button and the Set Auto Fill Time tool panel appears (Figure 106).

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 90](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-090.webp)

*Page 90 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 91

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 86 |
Figure 106. Set Auto Fill Time Tool Panel
The two auto fill times are displayed. You can schedule these for any times that work 
best for you, but there cannot be more than 16 hours between scheduled fills. The time 
between fills is displayed below the text fields.
Generally, it is recommended that these auto fill times be set for outside of normal 
working hours. The first fill of the day is typically a couple of hours before work begins 
on the NanoFab, as this allows time for the gun temperature to return to normal after 
the end of the fill.
If a fill time is changed, it may not take full affect right away. To avoid an interval that is 
too long or too short between fills, the software may wait a couple of fill cycles before 
putting the new fill times into effect. After entering the new fill time, click on the Save 
button.
4.8.2
Dewar Fill Command
In the middle of the Dewar Control tool panel is the Dewar Fill section with the Fill Now 
button (Figure 105). Click this button to start the Dewar fill right away. Do not click on 
this button unless there is a reason to fill the Dewar at that immediate time. Executing 
a Fill Now disrupts the normal auto fill times and it takes a few auto fill cycles before 
the fills are back to the scheduled times.
4.8.3
Active Cooling Status
The Active Cooling section of the Dewar Control tool panel displays the status, 
enabled or disabled, of the auto fill process. When auto fill is enabled, the word 
Enabled is displayed next to Active Cooling at the top of the section, and a message 
below that states that the system will be maintained in the cold state without any action 
by the operator.(Figure 215).

The purpose of this feature is to allow the operator to put the auto fill into Service mode 
while performing any service on the external LN2 source. The most common reason 
for entering Service mode is to replace the external LN2 source tank. It is 
recommended that you enter Service mode while changing the external tank to

which case, the Dewar system could be exposed to air and moisture, which would

Figure 109. Servicing Section of Dewar Control Tool Panel
To enter Service mode, click the Begin Servicing button in the section at the bottom 
of the Dewar Control tool panel (Figure 109). When you are finished servicing, be sure 
to click the Done Servicing button. Since the Dewar does not fill while in servicing 
mode, the Dewar may run out of LN2 if the tool is left in servicing mode for an 
extended period, and the gun will warm up. As a reminder that auto fill is in the 
servicing mode, the word Servicing, with a yellow flashing background, is displayed 
next to the Dewar Control icon.
Figure 110. Servicing Icon
4.8.5
Emergency Fill
If the Dewar bottom temperature should rise above 80 K (gun temperature 
approximately 100 to 105 K), the software initiates an emergency fill. An emergency fill 
is not triggered if the Dewar auto fill is in servicing mode. 
Note:
The HV power shuts off if the gun temperature reaches 120 K.
4.9
Tuning and Operating the Flood Gun
Please refer to "Flood Gun" on page 27 regarding the theory and operation of the flood 
gun.
Verify Flood Gun Not Shorted
Before powering up the flood gun, make sure that the ET detector grid is NOT shorted.
Note:
For the flood gun to function properly, the electrons emitted from the flood 
gun MUST be sufficiently repelled away from the ET detector to prevent 
saturation and damage to the detector’s scintillator. In normal operation, 
the ET grid voltage is switched to about -1000V, creating a field to repel 
the flood gun electrons. However, if the ET grid has been damaged or 
shorted to ground (for example, from being bumped by a sample), the 
repelling field may not be sufficient.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 93](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-093.webp)

*Page 93 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

| 89 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2

To verify normal operation of the ET grid (NOT shorted) perform the following steps:
1.
Continuously image, and zoom in to about 5 µm Field of View (FOV).
2.
Focus and stigmate the beam and set the contrast for medium-gray average 
intensity.
3.
In the ET Detector control, overwrite the 500V in the grid text field to 0 and press 
Enter.
4.
Observe the image; the features in the image should move to the right and down 
slightly and the image should begin to darken.
5.
Once this behavior has been verified, set the grid back to 500V (the image should 
quickly snap back to the original position and brightness).
6.
If changing the ET grid voltage has no effect on the image (position or brightness), 
DO NOT POWER UP THE FLOOD GUN.
Perform Emission Current Adjustment
Note:
This should be required only once unless the flood gun is replaced. Use 
this emission current adjustment only if the flood gun has been replaced 
and/or if you suspect this procedure has not been performed or the 
emission current has changed.
1.
Set the emission current of the flood gun to 1 to 2 µA. 
2.
From the Operation Center, power the flood gun on and keep it on for about an 
hour to stabilize.
3.
Power down the ET detector and wait 2 minutes for the voltages to ramp down.
4.
Go to the Inspector Manager and open BhvFloodGun, PolarisFloodGun, FGF-
GEmissionI:
Figure 111. Inspector Manager

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 94](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-094.webp)

*Page 94 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 90 |
5.
Look at the readback current (in Amps; should be in the -8 range).
6.
Set the Flood Gun Mode to On (this will unblank the flood gun and will only work if 
the ET is powered down so that the electrons from the flood gun do not damage 
the ET scintillator).
7.
Look at the readback current and adjust the filament voltage so that the emission 
current reads between 1 and 2E-6A.
8.
Set the Flood Gun Mode back to Auto (or Line or Frame).
9.
Power up the ET detector.
Background
There are two basic modes of charge neutralization on the Orion NanoFab – frame 
and line. Frame neutralization is only relevant for continuous imaging since it waits for 
one full image (or frame) to be completed before neutralizing. Line neutralization, on 
the other hand, will neutralize between each line of an image. For example, if the pixel 
resolution is set to 512, there will be 512 iterations of neutralization for a single image. 
Line neutralization is, therefore, much more effective than frame neutralization but will 
also be much slower. Frame neutralization can be used while navigating on a sample 
to find a specific area of interest, for example, since a faster response time is needed 
in this case. While the neutralization may not be as efficient, it may be adequate at 
lower magnifications since the charge density from the ion beam is less.
The most effective use of neutralization is line neutralization combined with line 
averaging. In this case, the neutralization is activated between each line that is 
averaged and between each line of the image. When used with short dwell times, the 
sample has much less chance to build up charge. For example, a 1024 pixel resolution 
image with 16 line averages, will have 1024 x 16 = 16384 cycles of neutralization for a 
single image.
In some cases, it may be desirable to use frame averaging on a charging sample while 
maximizing the neutralization. In this special case, the flood gun neutralization mode 
cannot be set to Auto. Line neutralization mode must be selected and frame 
averaging.
Optimize Flood Gun Emission
Optimize the flood gun emission onto the sample at a given working distance. 
Note:
It is recommend that the sample be placed at the beam coincidence/tilt 
eucentric working distance (usually between 8.5 and 9.0 mm). Optimizing 
the neutralization involves imaging on a charging sample to induce some 
charging but not too much and adjusting the flood gun’s x and y deflectors 
and focus to maximize the signal. The values in the following steps are 
guidelines; other settings can also be used.
1.
Set sample to the tilt eucentric and/or Ga FIB coincidence point working distance.
2.
Set the Flood Gun Mode to Frame.
3.
Set the Flood Time to 3000 usec.
4.
Set the Flood Gun Mode to Line.
5.
Set the Flood Time to 100 usec.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 95](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-095.webp)

*Page 95 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 96

| 91 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
6.
Set the Flood Gun Mode to Auto.
7.
Begin continuously imaging at 256 or 512 pixel resolution and lowest magnifica-
tion (largest FOV) possible. Use a dwell time of 1 µsec and no averaging.
8.
Adjust contrast for medium gray.
9.
Slowly zoom in until the image begins to darken.
10. Adjust the y and x deflectors to maximize the brightness of the image.
11. Change the Scan Parameters to Line averaging (about 8 lines); this will automat-
ically change the flood gun to line neutralization mode.
12. Repeat the previous two steps to optimize the neutralization.
13. Adjust the Focus slider for best neutralization (default is -433V).
Figure 112. Flood Gun tool panel
Note:
The behavior of neutralization in Auto mode is the following:
–
If Image Averaging is set to Frame, neutralization will be set to Frame.
–
If Image Averaging is set to Line, neutralization will be set to Line.
–
If no Image Averaging is used, neutralization will be set to Frame.
14. If the image is excessively bright on the left edge, increase the Grid Blank Time.
–
Adjustments can be made to the Flood Energy but should not exceed 900 
V and will likely require readjustment of the deflectors.
–
Adjustments to the source voltage will also affect the amount of neutral-
ization but should only be done after optimizing the deflectors and focus 
voltage. Do not exceed 2.50 V.
–
Adjustments can also be made to the Flood Energy but should not exceed 
850 V and will likely require readjustment of the deflectors.
–
Changing the magnification has a large impact on the neutralization since 
it dramatically affects the charge density of the ion beam on the sample.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 96](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-096.webp)

After optimizing the neutralization at a given sample working distance and flood gun

click the Create button to create a new preset.

Uncheck all of the categories except the Flood Gun.

Flood Gun at Coincident WD).
5.
Close the preset editor.

5
Tool Setup & Maintenance
5.1
Basic System Checks
A number of quick status checks of the tool can be made by viewing the Vacuum 
Dashboard. It is a good idea to make these checks when first sitting down at the tool, 
and also when the tool doesn’t seem to be operating correctly.
If all of these basic checks seem fine but the tool is still not functioning properly, see 
Section 7, Basic Troubleshooting.
The following checks assume that the tool is in standby mode, but otherwise 
operational. These checks would be typical before bringing up the tool for a working 
session. All of these checks can be made by observing the various readbacks in the 
main section of the Vacuum Dashboard (see Section 7.1, The Vacuum Dashboard).
1.
GFIS Gun Check
The state of the GFIS column should be Pumped. The gun pressure should be <1E-9 
Torr.
Gun Temperature should be in the range of 73 K to 85 K.
2.
Column Check
The GFIS Column pressure should be <1E-8 Torr.
3.
Chamber Check
The state of the chamber should be Pumped. The chamber pressure should be 
<3E-7 Torr.
4.
Dewar Check
The Dewar should be in the Maintain Cool state. Dewar bottom temperature should 
be <60 K.
The following checks can be done in the UI.
5.
High Voltage Check
Accelerator, Extractor, and Lens 1 voltage should be enabled. The extraction field 
should be at 105% of He BIV.  For a system with BIV at 35kV, this would be 36.75kV.
Note:
In the Zen UI, enabled is usually indicated by white text in the write field, 
and disabled by gray text, but the Extractor is an exception, which 
displays in gray text in Normal imaging mode, even when the high voltage 
is technically enabled.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 99](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-099.webp)

5.2
Basic Source Maintenance
Note:
The daily use of the system may often NOT require any GFIS source 
maintenance. You may cycle between Standby and Power up several 
times before any GFIS source maintenance is required.
Before starting any of the source maintenance procedures, it is best to position a 
conductive featureless sample under the beam.
“Source Maintenance” refers to a number of actions that are taken to obtain and align 
a trimer. There are a number of wizards that assist you in performing these actions. 
They are located under GFIS Source Maintenance, which is found from the main 
menu at the top of the UI (). To get to these wizards select Maintenance > GFIS 
Column > GFIS Source Maintenance.

to which the source is regulated. Shortly after reaching this temperature, a field 
(voltage potential between accelerator and extractor) is applied to the tip. The 
background in the graph becomes red again to indicate the presence of the field 
voltage.
Figure 124. Form the Source Display
After a little time, the source is set to a lower temperature and regulated there (Figure 
125). The image from the source view camera becomes noticeably less bright. Also, 
the time scale of the graph is occasionally adjusted based on how long the process 
has been running. Notice the difference in the time scale between Figure 124 and Figure 
125.

5.2.4
Select a Trimer
There are two situations in which this process is used. One is when the Form the 
Source process has just been completed. The other is when you just want to select a 
new trimer form the existing build (pyramid). As described below, some steps are 
skipped, depending on which situation applies.
Note:
For more information on a trimer, see 11.1, The Trimer.
Figure 129. GFIS Source Maintenance Wizard
The Select a Trimer tool panel opens with every function in this panel disabled except 
the check box for Ensure column is over sample. During the Select a Trimer routine, a 
beam is generated from the source. In order to see the source in SFIM mode it is 
necessary for the beam to strike a sample (conductive and preferably featureless) and 
generate secondary electrons that can be collected by the ET detector. 
Note:
“Featureless” refers to an area that does not have a pattern such as a 
grid. Bare metal, such as that on the sample holder, works best (see Figure 
130). A bare SEM stub works as well.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 110](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-110.webp)

Extractor is somewhat of a misnomer in this and other tool panels in the

The values displayed in the Extractor fields in the UI are in actuality 
extraction field values.
4.

for first light. In this case, leave the Set Extractor and Accelerator Voltages 
check box checked and click on the Begin button. The software automatically 
ramps the accelerator voltage up to 17,500 volts and the extractor field up to -
20,000 V (see Figure 134). Skip step 5 and proceed to step 6. 
5.

down to the next shelf of the existing pyramid, without rebuilding the pyramid 
(forming a source), you will want the accelerator and extractor voltages to remain 
at their current values. In this case, uncheck the Set Extractor and Accelerator 
Voltages check box, click the Begin button, and then skip to step 19.

Mechanical Tilt Alignment
1.
Adjust lens 1 until the image forms a circle - referred to as “at crossover”.
Figure 155. Beam At Crossover
2.
Increase lens 1 above crossover to see three separate atoms of the trimer as 
shown in Figure 156 and select the atom that appears to be brightest to align. Any 
atom can be used, although if one is clearly brighter it is recommended you use 
that one, but once selected you must keep track of that atom. 
3.
Make a note of the lens 1 voltage value displayed in the SFIM Controls tool panel.
Note:
Round this value to the nearest thousand if it is easier to remember.
Figure 156. Above Crossover Voltage

it was in step 2 and identify the same atom at the mirrored image. This value is 
referred to as “below crossover”.
Note:
As stated in section 11.13, Crossover, the image of the trimer above and 
below crossover are mirror images of each other on both axes.
5.
Make a note of the lens 1 voltage value displayed in the SFIM Controls tool panel.
Note:
Round this value to the nearest thousand if it is easier to remember.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 122](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-122.webp)

Set Source Distance
Use the Set Source Distance process to calibrate the distance from the source to lens 
1. 
Note:
In order for the column optics software to correctly determine the working 
distance, it needs to know a number of important values.  Many of these

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 126](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-126.webp)

*Page 126 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*
