5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 122 |
are the current operating voltages of the column (e.g., accelerating 
voltage), but one key piece of information is the distance from the source 
tip to lens 1.  Based on knowledge of this distance, the accelerating 
voltage and the lens 1 focus voltage, the software can calculate the 
position of the column crossover.  Using this crossover position, the 
accelerating voltage and the lens 2 focus voltage, the software can 
calculate the working distance. The basic algorithm is to use lens 1 to 
focus the beam at a known location in the column.  The value of the 
source distance (object for lens 1) will then be adjusted such that 
crossover position (image formed by lens 1) calculated by the optics is 
equal to the known location.

5.3
Aperture Selection and Alignment
5.3.1
Homing the Aperture Bar
Before an aperture can be selected, the aperture bar must be homed. The homed 
state of the aperture bar is displayed in the GFIS Column tool panel to the far right of 
Aperture. See yellow circle in Figure 169. If the state is Not Homed, the aperture must 
be homed before selecting it. If the state is shown as Homed, you can select it (see 
5.3.2, Selecting an Aperture).
Figure 169. Aperture Not Homed
1.
To home the aperture bar, select Aperture Home from the Maintenance > GFIS 
Column tool panel.
Figure 170.Start Home Routine
Click on Aperture Home in the Aperture tool panel to start the homing routine. After a 
short wait a popup appears stating that the apertures have been properly homed.
Figure 171. Message that Aperture is Homed
The state of the aperture bar that is displayed in the GFIS Column tool panel is now 
reported as Homed.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 129](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-129.webp)

*Page 129 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 130

| 125 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Figure 172. GIFS Column Tool Panel
5.3.2
Selecting an Aperture
To select an aperture, click the arrow in the GFIS Column tool panel to the left of 
Aperture to open the Aperture tool control and then use the arrow to the right of the 
aperture field to open the aperture drop-down list. Then click on an aperture to select 
it. The type and size of the selected aperture appears in the Aperture text field. At first 
it is grayed-out while the aperture moves into position. Once the aperture is in position 
the label is highlighted.
Figure 173. Aperture List
The apertures listed are those that are installed on your system. The list shown (Figure 
173) is for a GFIS column supplied with both helium and neon. The apertures listed as 
He apertures (2 -7) are made of thin gold foil. They are fragile and should be used only 
with the helium beam as they are easily damaged by the neon beam. The neon 
apertures (1 and 8-14) are much more robust and can be used with either neon or 
helium.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 130](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-130.webp)

*Page 130 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

5.3.3
Electronic Tilt and Shift Alignment
This section describes electronic fine alignment of the beam (using the quadrupoles) 
to an aperture using the Tilt/Shift knobs on the knob panel. If earlier mechanical 
alignment is done well, only small adjustments of the Tilt/Shift knobs are required to 
fine-tune the alignment. This is referred to as electronic tilt and shift alignment. If a 
large adjustment in the electronic tilt or shift is needed, a mechanical adjustment of the 
aperture or the gun head may be required (Section 5.3.3, Electronic Tilt and Shift 
Alignment).
The X and Y Tilt/Shift knobs on the knob panel are used for this alignment and the 
Mode button is used to toggle between tilt alignment and shift alignment. The mode 
that is currently selected is displayed at the bottom right side of the UI (Figure 174).
Figure 174. Current Mode Indication
The tilt alignment is done in SFIM mode and the shift alignment is done in Normal 
Imaging mode. These two alignments, tilt and shift, have some interaction with each 
other, therefore the process involves going back and forth between the two.
Electronic Tilt Alignment
1.
For the tilt alignment, select SFIM mode. 
2.
Use the Mode button to select Gun Tilt mode.
3.
Use the X and Y tilt knobs to position the selected atom of the trimer in the center 
of the cross hairs in the UI image field.
Note:
Imaging should be no lower than 512 pixel resolution and no shorter than 
2 usec dwell time.
Electronic Shift Alignment
1.
For shift alignment, go to Normal Imaging mode and focus on a feature with 
sharp edges, preferably somewhat round in shape so there are sharp edges on 
both axes.
Tilt/Shift X
Tilt/Shift Y
Mode Toggle

In a well-aligned system all four of these values (X shift, Y shift, X tilt, Y tilt) are within 
the range of ±200 µrad. If any of these values are outside of that range, mechanical 
adjustments must be made. If beam shift values are out of this range, the mechanical 
position of the aperture must be adjusted. If beam tilt values are outside of this range, 
a mechanical adjustment to the gun shift position is required. See “Electronic Tilt and 
Shift Alignment” on page 126.
5.3.4
Mechanical Gun Shift Alignment
Mechanical shift alignment of the gun head is necessary when the electronic beam tilt 
value becomes too large.
Mechanical gun shift alignment is done in SFIM mode. 
1.
Set the X and Y beam tilt values to zero first. To do this, Click the arrow to the left 
of Beam Tilt in the GFIS Column tool panel to open the Beam Tilt tool panel.
2.
Enter 0 in both the X and Y Beam Tilt fields (Figure 179).
Figure 179. Enter Gun Shift Values
3.
Switch to SFIM mode to display the trimer. 
4.
Position the selected atom of the trimer to the center of the cross hairs in the UI 
image field using the X and Y Gun Shift knobs on the GFIS column.

Mechanical Aperture Alignment
Coarse (mechanical) alignment of an aperture is necessary when the electronic beam 
shift value is too large. See the previous section for the procedure for electronic shift

Mechanical alignment is done in normal imaging mode. Before beginning the 
mechanical alignment of the aperture, set the X and Y beam shift values to zero. 
1.

Focus on a feature with sharp edges and somewhat round in shape so there are

left of Lens 2 Wobble. The image in the UI rotates by 90°, to align the X/Y scan of

4.
Lens 2 wobble sweeps the lens 2 voltage above and below the focus point value. 
If the beam is not in the center of lens 2, sweeping of the lens 2 voltage causes 
the image to wobble (back and forth movement of the feature) as it goes in and 
out of focus. 
5.
The four arrows below lens 2 Wobble (encircled in yellow in Figure 182) are a vir-
tual joystick used to move the aperture bar. Use these arrows to minimize the 
wobble. The X arrows affect horizontal image motion as viewed in the UI image 
and the Y arrows affect vertical motion in the UI image. The magnitude of aperture 
movement for each click is determined by the value entered in the Step Size field. 
If lens 2 wobble causes a large movement in the image, use a value in the range 
of 10 µm, and for fine adjustment use 1 µm. (see Section 8.11, Aperture Control for 
a description of the movements of the virtual joystick). 
6.
When the movement of the image has been minimized, uncheck the Lens 2 Wob-
ble check box and then click the Save button. This saves the current position of 
the aperture.

5.4
Focus and Stigmate the Beam
During normal operation you will be checking the focus and stigmation of the beam 
quite often. 
Whenever beam limiting aperture, spot control, or acceleration voltage is changed:
1.
Perform electronic beam tilt if necessary:
a)
Select aperture to be used and spot control to be used (typically 4 to 6).
b)
SFIM imaging, center atom of choice in screen.
c)
Fine tune using L1 wobble on Small setting and normal imaging.
2.
Perform electronic beam shift:
a)
Select aperture to be used and spot control to be used (typically 4 to 6).
b)
Normal imaging, remove L2 wobble down to about a 500nm FOV with L2 
wobble set to Small.
c)
Iterate L1 wobble with electronic tilt adjustment and L2 wobble with elec-
tronic shift adjustment until both L1 and L2 wobbles are minimized.
Note:
The electronic shift/align and electronic tilt values should be less than 500 
µrad in magnitude when complete; typically these values are 
approximately 200 µrad or less.  One exception is when the accelerating 
voltage is changed by a “large” amount – for example, when changing 
from 30 to 10 kV.  In this case the electronic tilt adjustment required to 
illuminate the aperture properly may be in the range of 300-400 µrad.
3.
Focus and stigmate the beam.
Note:
The knobs for focus and stigmation adjustments have sensitivities that 
vary with magnification/FOV.  At higher magnification/smaller FOV, the 
focus and stigmator knobs have a smaller voltage per turn than at lower 
magnification/larger FOV.  For that reason, you will be less likely to miss 
the “sweet spot” when turning the knob at higher magnification if you turn 
the knob a little too far.  Therefore, it is recommended to zoom in as much 
as possible when focusing and stigmating.  The optimum focus and 
stigmation is not affected by the magnification, so zooming in or out after 
optimizing does not require refocusing or restigmating.
5.4.1
Focus the Beam
Everyone has their own preferences as to which scan parameters to use when 
focusing the beam. Below are some basic tips to use when focusing.
Generally, it is best to find a feature relatively close to your area of interest, which has 
sharp edges and with a somewhat rounded shape. This gives you sharp edges in all 
directions. It is sometimes difficult to focus and stigmate on a one-dimensional feature 
like a line.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 136](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-136.webp)

*Page 136 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 137

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 132 |
Figure 183. Image with Good Focusing Features
Most people prefer to use a short dwell time to have a faster refresh time of the image. 
However this also reduces the signal to noise ratio. Using a reduced scan size (see 
Reduced button in the knob panel Section 3.10, Operator Console reduces the image 
refresh time as you are scanning a smaller area of the image, and therefore there are 
fewer pixels in the image. With this reduced size you do not have to use a shortened 
dwell time to speed up the refresh time.
While focusing the image you may notice indications of astigmatism of the beam. The 
section below explains how to stigmate (remove any astigmatism from) the beam.
5.4.2
Stigmating the Beam
See Section 11.4, Astigmatism, for an explanation of astigmatism.
When the beam is showing indications of astigmatism, the X and Y Stigmator controls 
on the knob panel (Figure 184) can be used to eliminate the affect. Following are some 
basic tips on stigmating the beam:
–
When stigmating, use the same feature that was used when focusing.
–
Before adjusting the Stigmator knobs on the knob panel, set the Focus 
knob to the best compromise between the X direction distortion and the Y 
direction distortion. When fine tuning the stigmation you may notice that 
when you have the sharpest focus on edges in the X direction, the Y 
direction edges are out of focus, and vise versa. Before adjusting the Stig-
mator knobs, set the focus to halfway between the best focus for X and 
the best focus for Y.
–
Adjust the X and Y Stigmator knobs to get the best focus on your feature.
–
Adjust focus between stigmating each axis. In other words; focus, stig-
mate X, focus, stigmate Y, focus...etc.

6
A Work Session
6.1
Daily Checks
It is a good idea to record a few system parameters at the beginning of each work day 
before starting work on the tool. A log or spreadsheet of these parameters should be 
maintained. This data could serve as an early warning of possible problems with the 
tool. The log would also be helpful to service personnel in the event of a problem with 
the tool.
Figure 185. Example of Daily Log
6.1.1
Perform Pre Gas-On Checks
Do a quick visual check of the tool by performing the basic system checks as 
described in Section 5.1, Basic System Checks.
Before turning on any gases record the base pressure in the gun, mid-column, and the 
work chamber. Also record the gun temperature and the Dewar bottom pressure.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 139](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-139.webp)

*Page 139 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 140

| 135 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
6.1.2
Power Up
The following steps assume that a sample has been loaded into the work chamber and 
that it is in the beam path. It is best if the area to be imaged is featureless. Imaging on 
an area with a lot of contrast makes the following checks more difficult.
Bring up the Operation Center by clicking on the Operation Center icon at the top of 
the UI.
Figure 186. Operation Center Icon
The Operation Center tool panel opens. If the system was previously put in Standby 
mode, the Operation Center looks like Figure 187.
Figure 187. Operation Center Tool Panel
In the GFIS Column section, click the Power Up button. The status of the GFIS 
column, displayed in the upper right corner of the GFIS Column section, changes from 
Standby to PoweringUp. Wait for the status to change to PoweredUp. This takes a 
minute or so. During this process, the helium gas is turned on and the column isolation 
valve opens.
If the ET detector is powered down, click the Power Up button in the ET Detector 
section. The status of the ET detector changes immediately to PoweredUp.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 140](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-140.webp)

*Page 140 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

6.1.3
Check the Trimer
Before beginning work you must check that a trimer is present and aligned. Go to 
SFIM imaging mode.
Figure 188. Trimer
Verify that the trimer is present and the atom of choice is aligned on the cross hairs in 
the UI. If one or more of the trimer atoms are missing you should try to get a new trimer 
from the current build. To do this, see Section 5.2.4, Select a Trimer. If you are not able to 
get another trimer from this build, see Section 5.2.3, Form the Source.
6.1.4
Align the Source
Whether you have a new trimer or are using a trimer that already existed, it is a good 
idea to go through the source align steps. See Section 5.2, Basic Source Maintenance and 
perform the steps of determining BIV, aligning the source, and setting the source 
distance.
6.2
Working Configuration
The next few sections describe the steps required to begin working. The selections 
made in some of these steps are determined by the application. The details about 
which selections to make are beyond the scope of this manual.
6.2.1
Load Sample
If the daily checks have just been performed, there is already a beam down and an 
aligned aperture in place. If your work sample is not already loaded into the chamber, 
do that now. See Section 4.3, Loading and Unloading Samples.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 141](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-141.webp)

*Page 141 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 142

| 137 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
6.2.2
Set Landing Energy
Landing energy is the energy at which ions hit the sample, determined by the 
accelerator voltage. Set the landing energy using the Landing Energy control in the 
GFIS Column tool panel. Generally set it around 25 to 30 kV. See 8.10, Landing Energy 
Control.
:
Figure 189. Landing Energy
For helium operation set the extractor to 100% of BIV. See Section 5.2.5, Determine BIV 
for details on how BIV is determined.
6.2.3
Select Aperture
Decide which aperture to use, based on your application. Gold foil apertures are 
designated as helium apertures. Molybdenum apertures are designated as neon 
apertures. Do not use the gold foil apertures when running neon, as they are easily 
damaged by neon. 
Select the aperture through the Aperture control in the GFIS Column tool panel. See 
sections 5.3.1, and 5.3.2 for more detail on apertures and aperture selection.
Smaller apertures can be helpful in getting higher resolution images, but also result in 
less beam current, leading to noisier images. Consult your tool owner for further 
information. 
Note:
Changing the aperture causes large changes in ion beam current: 
Doubling the aperture size quadruples the beam current.
General usage:
–He 5 µm: UHR imaging
–He 10 µm: HR imaging
–Ne 20 µm: Imaging and sub 30 nm patterns
–Ne 40 µm: Small patterns (sub 300 nm)
–Ne 70 µm: Large area patterning (larger than 
300 nm)
Figure 190. Select Aperture

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 142](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-142.webp)

*Page 142 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 143

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 138 |
Select Spot Control Value
Select the required spot control value using the Spot Control tool in the GFIS Column 
tool panel. See Section11.14, Spot Control for a discussion of spot control.
Note:
Changing the spot control value changes the ion beam current (the 
number of ions that interact with the sample per second). Reducing the 
spot control by 1 number doubles the ion beam current, and thus 
increases beam size: If spot 5 produces 1 pA beam current, spot 4 
produces 2 pA beam current
6.3
Imaging
6.3.1
Setting Parameters
The following imaging parameters must be set before recording an image:
–
Contrast and Brightness
–
Field of View (FOV)
–
Scan Size (Pixel Resolution) 
–
Dwell time (Scan Speed)
–
Averaging

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 143](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-143.webp)

*Page 143 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 144

| 139 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Contrast and Brightness
1.
Select a medium speed dwell time (1µs).
2.
Reduce the contrast to zero and adjust the brightness to a level so the last gray 
level can be seen, by eye, before the screen goes black (50% is a good default 
selection).
3.
Increase the contrast so that the signal level shows an image.
Field of View (FOV)
Select a FOV appropriate for the size of the area of interest on the sample. Typically, 
this is done with the Magnification knob on the knob panel. There are some FOV 
related tools in the UI that can also be used (see 8.4, FOV (Field of View) Control).
Scan Size (Pixel Resolution)
Scan size sets the amount of scan points (pixels) in the image. For general imaging, 
512 x 512 or 1024 x 1024 typically works best. When making high a resolution image, 
1024 x 1024 or 2048 x 2048 are the preferred selections. 
Note: If you are using low magnification, you may need a high pixel resolution a 
pixelated image.
Dwell Time (Scan Speed)
Dwell time sets the time that the ion beam stays at a single scan point. The longer the 
dwell time, the slower the scan speed.
Note:
The values below are suggested values only. The required scan time is 
sample-dependent. Increase the dwell time (slower scan speed) until a 
smooth (non-noisy) image is obtained. Be careful not to damage your 
sample. For charging samples and beam-sensitive samples use short 
dwell times in combination with averaging functions. 
Note:
Suggested values:
–
0.5 µs: wobble
–
1 µs: alignments
–
>10 µs: images

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 144](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-144.webp)

*Page 144 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 145

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 140 |
Averaging
Averaging is typically used in combination with short dwell times for charging samples 
or beam-sensitive samples. The mode selects which type of averaging is used and the 
number selects how many lines or frames are averaged.
–
Off: No averaging is used
–
Line: Each line is scanned the selected number of times and the average 
signal for each pixel is displayed.
–
Frame: Each frame is scanned the selected number of times and the 
average signal is displayed.
6.3.2
Recording and Saving Images
Recording an Image
–
Press Stop button to stop imaging
–
Press Grab button to capture the image
Capturing Images
After obtaining a good quality image, there are 3 format options:
•
EM.tif – Electron Microscope. This is the most commonly used format and cap-
tures “tiftag” data as well (many system settings and parameters that the image 
was captured with).  This data can be accessed with the NanoFab’s 
CZTifTagViewer program.
•
Standard.tiff – This format captures the image only and not the data or tiftags.
•
CZI (Carl Zeiss Internal) is for use across Zeiss products like SEMs and light 
microscopes.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 145](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-145.webp)

6.4
Ending a Work Session
6.4.1
Unloading Sample
Note:
If no one else is using the tool, you can leave the sample and holder inside 
the chamber to minimize exposure to atmosphere.
1.
Bring up the sample management tab.
2.
Press the Exchange button.
–
Brings sample to exchange 
position
–
Closes the CIV
–
Turns off ET detector, flood gun 
and lens 2
–
Opens the air lock valve
3.
Pull the sample out via the transfer rod.
–
Screw to the sample via screw-
ing knob (clockwise).
–
Hold white seal while pulling 
back the rod as this can come 
out while pulling.
4.
Press the Vent button when in loading 
position (Store green light is on).
–
Automatically closes air lock valve
5.
Remove sample holder and samples.
6.
Store sample holder in a clean, dry environment away from exposure to dust (a 
closed storage box, preferably with nitrogen purge, is ideal.
6.4.2
Switching Mode to Standby
The microscope typically remains powered up at all times. That means the sample 
chamber is under vacuum, the ion source is at cryogenic temperature, and all of the 
control electronics are operating and the HVPS is still on.
This section describes the procedure for setting the tool to Standby mode.
1.
Change to Standby mode.
Note:
Put the system into the standby mode when you are not going to use the 
tool for an extended period of time, for example, overnight.
Open the Operation Center and click Standby in the GFIS Column section as shown 
in Figure 191. The column is isolated, the helium gas turns off, the flood gun powers 
down, and the state is Standby. The extractor voltage increases to 105% of the helium 
BIV setting to help keep contaminants off of the source.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 146](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-146.webp)

*Page 146 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

Figure 191. Standby Mode
Note:
If the system reports Not Operating, the system is in an in-between state 
where imaging is not possible, for example, the gas is off or the column is 
isolated, but it is not in the Standby state.

7
Basic Troubleshooting
This section is intended to familiarize the operator with the use of the Vacuum 
Dashboard for use in troubleshooting, and to help an operator do some basic 
troubleshooting of the most common problems that can be encountered with the 
NanoFab. Sometimes the issue is simply a set-up or configuration problem that can be 
resolved without having to call for service.
7.1
The Vacuum Dashboard
This section gives a quick overview of the operational status of the NanoFab. The 
Vacuum Dashboard should be used to observe states, valve status, pressures, 
temperatures, etc., or to launch DataViewer but not to run the NanoFab.  With very few 
exceptions, the normal operation of the NanoFab can and should be done through the 
Zen UI and not the from the dashboard.  The dashboard should be “look but don’t 
touch” unless troubleshooting.
The server must be running to access it. Go to the tools menu in System Manager and 
select Vacuum Screen (Figure 192).
Figure 192. System Manager
The Vacuum Dashboard appears (Figure 193).

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 149](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-149.webp)

*Page 149 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 150

| 145 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Figure 193. Vacuum Dashboard
The larger display, on the left side of the Vacuum Dashboard, depends upon the 
configuration of the NanoFab in regard to its options. The configuration shown in Figure 
193 is a NanoFab with one ion column and two imaging gases. A glance at the left side 
of the Vacuum Dashboard gives you a pretty good assessment of the operational state 
of the NanoFab.
The panel on the right side of the Vacuum Dashboard has at least 4 tabs: GFIS 
Column, GFIS Gas Box, Dewar, and Air Lock & Chamber. If you have the Capella 
option there is also a Gallium FIB Column tab.
In Figure 193, 4 tabs are shown; 3 at the top and one at the bottom. 
Note:
Before a discussion of the Vacuum Dashboard, a general understanding 
of State Machine is helpful. State Machine is a conceptual model of a 
machine that can be in a finite number of states, transitions, and actions. 
This conceptual model was used in the programing of the Vacuum 
Dashboard. The overall system, when operated by the software, is in state 
machine mode. It consists of a number of state machine modules. The 
chamber, the air lock, the GFIS column, among others, are each designed 
on the state machine model. Therefore one action or transition, for 
example, within the operation of the chamber, will bring about other 
programmed transitions or actions within the chamber module. In a few 
instances, transitions will also occur in connecting state machines. For 
example, the action of venting the chamber will cause the action of

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 150](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-150.webp)

*Page 150 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 151

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 146 |
venting the air lock also, even though the chamber and the air lock consist 
of two state machine modules.
As an example of a state machine module, the chamber state machine 
allows a finite number of actions by the operator: vent, rough, or pump, 
producing the states of vented, rough pumped, and high vacuum pumped. 
Selecting any one of these puts a number of other components into 
actions, such as opening or closing valves or starting a pump, etc.
State machine is the normal operating mode for the operator. Diag. 
(diagnostic) is the operating mode for use by Zeiss Field Services, for 
maintenance of the tool. An operator should not vary from state machine 
mode, as he may cause some undesired consequences.
7.1.1
General Features
The general features of the Vacuum Dashboard are discussed below, followed by 
descriptions of individual readbacks and indicators.
Most of the modules displayed in the dashboard are under state machine control. The 
text displayed in purple is the current state of that module of the NanoFab. For 
example, in Figure 194, the current state of the chamber is shown to be Pumped. Each 
of the modules have only a few valid states they can be in. If the current state of 
components such as valves, pumps, gauges, etc., does not match a defined state 
exactly, the state is displayed as Undefined. When a section is in state machine mode, 
none of the valves or pumps that are a part of that module state machine can be 
individually controlled. The state of a module can only be changed by selecting 
another state from the list of valid commands.
Right-click any purple text to see a list of the 
valid commands of that section of the NanoFab. 
For example, Figure 194 shows the list of valid 
commands for the chamber, which are Pump, 
Vent, and Rough. Each list of valid commands 
also includes two other selections; Init 
(Initialize) and Diag (Diagnostic).
Figure 194. Chamber States
7.1.2
Diagnostic Mode
Selecting Diag takes that section out of state machine mode and puts it in Diag mode. 
The displayed state for that section is now Diag. In Diag mode, the engineer has more 
individual control of the valves and pumps. This can be dangerous. There are some 
interlocks preventing actions that could be harmful to the NanoFab in Diag mode, but 
it is possible to create some undesired effects. Do not use Diag mode unless you are 
a trained engineer or are instructed to do so by Zeiss personnel.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 151](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-151.webp)

*Page 151 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 152

| 147 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
It is not possible to go directly from Diag mode to any of the valid state machine states. 
The only way to exit Diag mode is to click on Init. This causes the software to check 
the various parameters and determine if all of them line up exactly with a particular 
defined state. If that is the case, that state displays. If not, Undefined is displayed.
7.1.3
Valve Control
Valves are represented in the Vacuum Dashboard as a blue circle with a white or gray 
bar running through the middle. On the left side of the Vacuum Dashboard shown in 
Figure 195, between the chamber and the GFIS column, is shown the column isolation 
valve (labeled Column Valve). Note the color of the bar and the position of the bar. 
When the bar is white, the valve is controllable by you. When the bar is gray the valve 
is not controllable by the operator.
Note:
Although the valve in Figure 195 may at first appear open, be aware that 
the beam is vertical, therefore the valve in this position blocks the beam.
Figure 195. Opening CIV
Right-click the valve and a popup appears with Open Valve and Close Valve as 
options (Figure 195). In this case, since the valve is closed, the Open Valve option is 
active and the Close Valve option is grayed out. If the valve were open, the reverse 
would be true. In the configuration shown in Figure 195, click Open Valve to open this 
valve. The valve now appears like this
.
If the position of the bar is lined up with the direction of flow, the valve is open. In Figure 
196, the two valves directly below the air lock are both closed - the bars are horizontal 
and the flows (the yellow lines in this case) are vertical. The other two valves are open 
since in both cases the bar is lined up with the direction of the yellow flow line at the 
point where the valve is placed.
Some valves are not controllable by the operator. The examples shown in Figure 196 in 
the Air Lock and Chamber panel are not controllable. Notice that the bar is gray for all 
four valves, meaning that none of the valves are controllable by the operator.

7.2
System Snapshot
The System Snapshot is a feature that triggers generation of a file that collects a large 
amount of data from the NanoFab. This data is useful to factory personnel for 
troubleshooting a problem that occurred with a NanoFab. If an unexplained event 
happens with the NanoFab, it is a good idea to create a system snapshot that can be 
sent to the factory for analysis. This file is too big to send via email, therefore a file 
transfer service must be used.
1.
Open Athena System Manager.
Figure 203. Athena System Manger Tools Tab
2.
Select Tools > System Snapshot. The System Snapshot status screen opens.
Figure 204. System Snapshot Status Screen.
3.
Click Start. The ReasonForm dialog box opens.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 156](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-156.webp)

*Page 156 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 157

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 152 |
Figure 205.The ReasonForm
4.
Enter the requested information.
Note:
The operator must enter his/her first and last name. All other functions, 
except the Cancel button, are disabled until the name is entered. Once a 
name is entered, all other functions, except the Continue button, are 
enabled. The Continue button is enabled after you enter the description 
into the text field.
Note:
If the problem occurred within the last few minutes, you can leave the 
default time and date in the corresponding fields. 
a)
If the problem occurred earlier, update the date and time field to the 
approximate time of the incident. 
b)
Then enter a detailed description of the observations of the problem. For 
example, state what the operator was doing at the time, what state the 
NanoFab was in, what was observed, etc. The more information you 
enter, the more help it is in finding a solution to the problem.
5.
Click Continue. The software beings collecting data. The progress bar tracks the 
percentage of completion.
Figure 206. Progress Bar

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 157](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-157.webp)

When the snapshot is complete, a popup appears, letting you know that the 
snapshot is complete.
The snapshot is automatically saved to the snapshots folder, which is located 
at C:\ProgramData > CarlZeiss > Polaris > Snapshots. However, the 
ProgramData folder is usually hidden so it may not be displayed in Windows 
Explorer. You can get to the Snapshots directory by typing the path into the

7.3
Controllers Not Communicating
There are a couple of features in Service Machine that can be used to check 
communication to all of the nodes and modules in the NanoFab.
7.3.1
Hardware Communication Status
If any of the modules are not communicating with the UI, there could be a problem with 
the system computer communicating to that module. For example, you are unable 
control functions on a module or do not get updated readbacks. 
1.
In the System Manager, select Tools > Service Machine.
2.
Select Hardware Communication Status from the menu on the left of the win-
dow. The Hardware Communications Status screen appears, providing a status 
indication of all the controllers in the NanoFab.
Figure 208. Hardware Communication Status

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 158](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-158.webp)

*Page 158 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 159

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 154 |
3.
Verify that there is a green bi-directional arrow next to all of the controllers. If any 
are not green, the system computer is not communicating to that controller. For 
example, in Figure 209, the NPVE is not communicating with the system computer.
Figure 209. NPVE Not Communicating
4.
Verify that the controller is powered on and verify that the communications cable 
to the controller is securely attached. 
5.
If that all checks out, power cycle the controller (turn off and turn on the rack that 
contains the board that’s not communicating), shut down the UI, and stop the 
server. Wait a couple of minutes after turning on the power to the controller and 
restart the server. 
Note:
If you need to power cycle rack 2, move the stage to a safe position (lower 
Z), as you will need to home the stage after powering back up.
Note:
For a description of rack 1 and rack 2, See “Electronics System” on 
page 41.
6.
If the bi-directional arrow is still not green, contact your service representative. 
7.3.2
Firmware Update
Another communication check can be done through the Firmware Update function, 
which is also accessed through the Service Machine menu. Select Tools > Service 
Machine > Firmware Update and the Firmware Update screen opens (Figure 210).

replaceable boards) in the system. This can be used as a test of the communication 
between the system computer and all of the subsystems in the NanoFab. Click the 
Query Nodes button in the upper right corner of the window. The system software 
goes out to each node and reads that node’s firmware version. If communications are 
open to all boards, the screen will look like the screen shown below in Figure 211, with

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 156 |
The purpose of running this test is to check the communication between the system 
computer and each node. If a red X appears next to a node name instead of a green 
check mark, there is one of two problems: either the system computer cannot 
communicate with the node, in which case the status displayed for that node is 
Unreachable – Could not ping node, or the firmware version for that node does not 
match the system software version, and you will get an Incompatible firmware 
message. If you get the Unreachable message, check that the node is powered up 
and the communications cable is securely attached. If it is still reporting unreachable, 
power cycle the node and then stop and re-start the server. If still reporting 
unreachable, contact your service representative. 
The Incompatible firmware message is an unlikely event for a working microscope as 
this can only happen if the module for that node has been replaced with in incorrect 
version. In the unlikely event that you do get this error, verify that the check box next to 
the red X is checked, then click the Download button at the bottom of the window. 
After the download completes, click Query Nodes. If the Incompatible Firmware 
message appears again, contact your service representative.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 161](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-161.webp)

7.4
Gun /Column Issues
Use this section for help in resolving issues with the gun and column. Figure 212 shows 
what the readbacks related to the gun and column should be, under normal operation. 
With the imaging gases off, the pressures and temperature should be approximately 
as shown. The state of the Dewar should be Maintain Cool. If any of these readings 
are not correct, using the basic troubleshooting tips in this section may help you 
resolve the problem without having to call your service representative.
Figure 212. Dewar in Normal Operation
7.4.1
Maintain Cool State
The Dewar should always be left in the Maintain Cool state. If it is not in this state, the 
Dewar does not automatically fill. To set the Dewar to the Maintain Cool state, right-
click inside the graphic of the Dewar to display the list of valid commands and select 
Maintain Cool (Figure 213).

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 162](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-162.webp)

*Page 162 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

Figure 213. Change State
If the Dewar system is operating properly, it will remain in the Maintain Cool state once 
you select it. If it will not go to the Maintain Cool state, nor stay in that state, 
troubleshooting is required. One reason could be that the Dewar Auto Fill is disabled 
(see next section).
7.4.2
Dewar Auto Fill Status
The Active Cooling section of the Dewar Control tool panel displays the status, 
enabled or disabled, of the auto fill process. To open the Dewar Control tool panel, 
click the Dewar Fill icon in the menu bar at the top of the UI. 
Figure 214. Dewar Fill Icon
When auto fill is enabled, the word Enabled is displayed next to Active Cooling at the 
top of the section, and a message below that states that the system will be maintained 
in the cold state without any action by the operator (Figure 215).
Figure 215. Active Cooling Enabled
If Active Cooling is enabled and the Dewar cannot be put into the Maintain Cool state, 
contact your service representative. If the Auto Fill is disabled, the words, Disabled- 
user action required, is displayed at the top of the Active Cooling section (Figure 216), 
and the reason is given in highlighted text.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 163](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-163.webp)

If the gun has warmed up too much, Active Cooling in the Dewar Control tool panel 
cannot be used to cool the gun. It must be done in the GFIS Column Maintenance.
If the gun is in the Pumped state select the Cool radio button in the Recipe section and 
then click Execute Now. This starts an automatic sequence to cool the gun.
7.4.4
Gun Temperature
In the upper right section of the Vacuum Dashboard are two temperature readings. 
One is the gun temperature and the other is the Dewar temperature (Figure 221).
Figure 221. Gun and Dewar Temperature
Under normal operating conditions, the gun is at a temperature of about 80K. If the 
gun temperature is above 85K, there could be a problem. During the Dewar fill 
process, the gun temperature could rise to as high as 95K. However, after the fill 
process is completed the gun temperature should return to normal operating 
temperature within an hour or so, which is normal. 
If the temperature rise is not due to a recent Dewar fill process, there is a problem. The 
gun is cooled by the Dewar. The normal Dewar temperature is about 60K. If not, it is 
likely that either the Dewar contents are not being pumped on or the Dewar is empty 
because it ran out of LN2. 
1.
If the Dewar temperature is above 77K, the Dewar is probably empty (see the next 
section, Dewar Empty).
2.
If the Dewar temperature is between 60K and 77K, there is probably LN2 in the 
Dewar but it is not being pumped on (see the section on the next page, Dewar Not 
Being Pumped).
7.4.5
Dewar Issues
Dewar Empty
If the Dewar is empty, the reason it didn’t fill needs to be determined and corrected.
a)
The tank valve may be closed. Check the valve and open it if necessary.
b)
The most likely reason that the Dewar would be empty is that the external 
fill tank is empty. If that is the case, replace the tank with a full one, using 
the procedure described by your tool owner. There are safety issues 
involved so this should only be done by trained personnel. After the tank

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 166](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-166.webp)

*Page 166 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 167

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 162 |
has been replaced, put the Dewar in the Maintain Cool state (see the ear-
lier section, Dewar State).
c)
Another reason the Dewar would not fill is if the gun is not in the pumped 
state. If the state of the gun, as displayed in the vacuum dashboard, is not 
Pumped, see Section 7.4.6, Gun/Column Pressure.
d)
If the external tank is not empty, there is some other reason the Dewar did 
not fill. Check whether the valve on the external tank is open. If the valve 
was closed, open it and then put the Dewar in the Maintain Cool state 
(see the earlier section, Dewar State).
e)
Another reason for the Dewar not filling could be that the fill circuit 
breaker is off. The state of this circuit breaker can be read in the Dewar 
panel on the right of the Vacuum Dashboard on the left hand side, just 
below the Dewar fill valve. Verify that the circuit breaker, CB1, on the LN2 
breaker panel on the back of the chase rack is in the On (up) position (Fig-
ure 223).
Figure 222. Fill Circuit Breaker On or Off
If the circuit breaker is Off, go to the chase rack and manually switch it to On.
Figure 223. Chase CB1 Circuit Breaker
 On 
 Off

Dewar Not Empty
1.
If the external tank is not empty, the valve on the tank is open, and CB1 is in the 
On position, but the Dewar is not in the Maintain Cool state, see the earlier sec-
tion, Dewar State. 
2.
If everything with the external tank is fine, CB1 is on, and the Dewar is in the 
Maintain Cool state, yet the gun is >77K, contact your service representative.
Dewar Not Being Pumped
Under typical operating conditions, the Dewar contents are pumped to lower the 
pressure in the vessel to about 2 Torr. This has the effect of lowering the boiling point 
of the LN2 from 77K to <60K. The lower temperature at the Dewar bottom results in a 
gun temperature of <80K. If the Dewar is not pumped on, the Dewar temperature rises 
to about 77K and the gun temperature rises to about 95K.
Use this section to resolve the problem of the Dewar not being pumped on. Under 
typical operating conditions, the valves and gauges in the Dewar panel of the Vacuum 
Dashboard look like those in Figure 224.
Figure 224. Dewar Panel Typical Conditions
1.
If the Dewar contents pressure gauge (near the center of the panel) is not reading 
in the range of 1-2 Torr, the Dewar may not be being pumped on. Verify that the 
Dewar rough valve is open and the Dewar rough pressure gauge indicator is 
green.
2.
If the valve is closed and the Dewar rough pressure gauge indicator is green, 
open the valve by right-clicking it and select Open Valve. The valve will be inhib-
ited from opening if the Dewar rough pressure gauge is indicator is red.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 168](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-168.webp)

*Page 168 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 169

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 164 |
3.
If the valve opened on command, the Dewar contents pressure should start going 
back down towards 2 Torr.
4.
If the Dewar rough pressure gauge indicator is red, it is likely that the Dewar rough 
pump is not running. Check the pump, normally located in the chase room, to see 
if it is running. 
5.
If the Dewar rough pump is running but the Dewar rough pressure gauge is indi-
cating in the red area, contact your service representative.
6.
If the pump isn’t running, try pushing the Start button on the top of the pump (Fig-
ure 225).
Figure 225. Rough Pump Panel
7.
If the pump does not start, contact your service representative. If it starts, continue 
to the next step.
8.
If the pump starts, wait for the Dewar rough pressure gauge indicator to turn green 
and then open the Dewar rough valve. The Dewar contents pressure will begin to 
drop until it reaches the 1 – 2 Torr range. The Dewar temperature should now start 
moving down towards 60K.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 169](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-169.webp)

7.4.6
Gun/Column Pressure
Use this section to resolve issues when the gun and/or the mid-column are not at their 
normal vacuum level. The pressure gauges for these, GFIS Gun Pressure and GFIS 
Column Pressure, can be read in the main section of the Vacuum Dashboard, just to 
the right of the graphic of the GFIS column. 
Figure 226. Gun and Column Pressure
1.
If the state of the GFIS column displayed is Pumped No Ion Pump, it means the 
ion pump is off and the GFIS Column Pressure gauge is grayed out. This is a nor-
mal state when helium is flowing. If helium is not flowing, go to the Pumped state.
Figure 227. List of Pump States
Select Pump and within a minute or so the state of the GFIS column should 
change to Pumped. If the state of the GFIS column does not change to Pumped, 
contact your service representative.
2.
If the state of the GFIS gun displayed is Vented, select Pump from the menu (Fig-
ure 227). This should start the automated process of pumping the column. The

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 170](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-170.webp)

state of the GFIS column changes to To Pump. It may take many minutes to for

3.

Undefined, click on Pump. If any error message appears, contact your service

7.5
Chamber Pressure Issues
1.
Use this section to resolve issues when the chamber is not at its normal vacuum 
level.The Chamber pressure gauge is below the chamber in the Vacuum Dash-
board (Figure 228). If it does not read <3E-7 Torr, and the chamber is in the 
Pumped state, or if the chamber does not reach the Pumped state, contact your 
service representative.
Figure 228. Chamber Pressure
.
Figure 229. Chamber State
2.
If the state displayed is Vented or Rough, right-click on the state displayed to open 
the states command menu, and select Pump. The state should change to To 
Pump, and then, after a number of minutes, the state should change to Pumped. If 
any error message appears, or if the chamber does not reach the Pumped state, 
contact your service representative.
3.
If the state of the chamber displayed is Diag, click on Init. If the state goes to 
Undefined, click on Pump. If any error message appears, contact your service 
representative.

7.6
Image Issues
Use this section to resolve imaging issues. It covers some basic checks and 
troubleshooting that may uncover the problem and a way to fix it without having to call 
your service representative. 
7.6.1
Basic Image Configuration
If no image can be obtained when you are expecting to obtain an image there are a 
few things that can be checked that might resolve the problem.
The typical Vacuum Dashboard when imaging with helium is shown in Figure 230. 
Using this as a reference, there are a number of quick things to check if not getting an 
image.
Figure 230. Typical Helium Parameters
In Figure 230 helium is being used as the imaging gas. For this section, for the sake of 
simplicity, it is assumed that helium is the selected imaging gas. 
1.
First, verify that the state of the gas box is Helium On. If the state is Gas Off, no 
imaging gas is flowing and you will not be able to get an image. If the gas is off, 
right-click Gas Off and the states menu appears (Figure 231), Select Helium On. 
The state changes to Working, and then after a minute or so, the state becomes 
Helium On.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 172](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-172.webp)

*Page 172 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 173

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 168 |
Figure 231. Gas States
When the state of the gas box is Helium On, verify that the GFIS gun pressure is at the 
set point. See Section 8.9, Gas Control.
2.
Set the aperture to Big Hole. See Section 5.3, Aperture Selection and Alignment.
3.
Verify that the column blanking mode is set to Autoblanking. See Section 8.20.3, 
Blanking Mode.
4.
Verify that there is a sample under the beam that will emit secondary electrons.
5.
Verify that beam scanning is on. If beam scanning is on, the middle button at the 
top of the Acquisition panel will have a spinning red circle around the word Stop, 
as shown in (Figure 232).
Figure 232. Beam Scanning On
6.
If beam scanning is off, the button displays the word Continuous (Figure 233).
Figure 233. Beam Scanning Off
No image is displayed on the UI if beam scanning is not on. Click on the 
Continuous button to begin scanning.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 173](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-173.webp)

If the conditions above have been met, the NanoFab is configured correctly for 
obtaining an image. The next few sections will check key hardware and check 
for a beam and other factors that may affect your image.
7.6.2
Checking the ET Detector
1.
In order to get an image, the ET detector must be enabled. To check whether the 
it is enabled, open the Operation Center tool panel by clicking on the Operation 
Center icon, located near the top of the UI.
Figure 234. Operation Center Icon
The Operation Center tool panel opens.
Figure 235. Operation Center Tool Panel
The state of the ET detector is displayed on the right side of this panel, directly 
across from the words ET Detector. It should be PoweredUp, with a green LED.
If the ET detector is not powered up, click the Power Up button. If you are unable 
to power up the ET detector, contact your service representative.
2.
Open the ET Detector tool panel and check the Show All check box in the upper 
right corner (Figure 236).

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 174](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-174.webp)

•
Verify that the Signal Processing control is set to about 23.3. 
Note:
The value is displayed only when the cursor hovers over the slide control.
•
To adjust the signal, move the slider to the left and right.
•
Start scanning while observing an active image window in the UI. 
•
Using the ET detector controls shown in Figure 236 do the following:
–
Set the Contrast to 0. 
–
Slide the Brightness control from the far left, all the way to the right and 
verify that the image in the UI goes from black to light gray. 
Note:
If this does not happen, the problem is most likely with the SAS or system 
computer.
•
If the Brightness control works as described above, set it to 50% and increase 
the Contrast control until noise is seen in the UI window as shown in Figure 237.
3.
The gain through the ET detector is controlled by the Contrast adjustment. The 
output from the ET detector is through the connector labeled Video and is fed over 
to the SAS assembly.
Figure 237. Detecting Noise

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 175](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-175.webp)

If the amount of noise in the display can be controlled by the Contrast setting, it 
indicates that the ET detector photo multiplier tube is functioning. It also confirms 
that the pre-amp is powered, functional, and the video output is connected to the 
SAS. 
4.
If noise is not present, the problem is most likely with the ET detector module. 
Contact your service representative.
5.
If noise is present, the symptom of no valid image implies that secondary elec-
trons are not making it into the ET detector assembly. The reasons for this could 
be:
–
The ET detector grid is not attracting secondary electrons. See the next 
section, Checking the ET Grid Power Supply to verify that the grid PS is work-
ing.
–
No secondary electrons are being generated, which could indicate that 
the ion beam is not striking a target. If all of the previous checks have 
been done, the most likely cause of no secondary electrons is that there 
is no ion beam. Go to Section 7.6.3, Checking for an Ion Beam.
Checking the ET Grid Power Supply
Open the ET Detector tool panel (Figure 238).Verify that the text box to the right of the 
Grid slider is reading 500V. If it isn’t, move the Grid slider all the way to the right. The 
text box should now read 500V. Note that this reading is only the commanded voltage, 
it is not a readback of the voltage. To get a readback of the grid voltage, place the 
cursor over the slider control and a readback appears at the bottom right.
Figure 238. ET Detector Tool Panel
There may also be a ghost image of the slider which is an indicator of the readback 
voltage. In Figure 239, notice the ghost slider at the left of the grid slider range. The 
ghost slider indicates that the grid voltage is close to zero. Place the cursor over either 
the control slider or the ghost slider to display the grid readback voltage close to zero 
at the bottom right.

In this case, try powering down the ET detector through the Operation Control tool 
panel (see Section 7.6.2, Checking the ET Detector) and then powering it back up. If the 
grid voltage is still not correct, contact your service representative.
7.6.3
Checking for an Ion Beam
If all the conditions in this section have been met and there is still no image, verify that 
there is a trimer. If you do not have a trimer but you can see some emission from the 
source, build a new trimer. See Section 5.2, Basic Source Maintenance.
Note:
Check the blanking aperture reading for beam current. Under typical 
conditions with Big Hole selected as the aperture, the blanking current 
should read at least a few pA and normally around 100 pA.
If there is no emission from the source, verify that the high voltages for the column are 
all enabled. Using the GFIS Column tool panel (Figure 240), verify that the Accelerator, 
Extractor, Lens 1 and Lens 2 readbacks are approximately as shown. If any of them 
are close to zero, that power supply is probably disabled.

8.1
Mode
The Mode linked menu allows you to select between Normal mode and Probe Edit. 
Generally, Normal mode is selected. Probe Edit mode is used only to edit entries in the 
probe table (Figure 244). 
Figure 244. Probe Edit
8.2
Probe Control
The concept of probes and the probe table is discussed in more detail in Section 9.2, 
Probe Tables. A brief description of the probe tools is given here.
A probe table is a collection of probes. A probe is defined as a set of column settings 
that configure a column for a specific beam current, landing energy, and beam shape.
To the right of Probe in the Probe control is a description of the current aperture, the 
acceleration voltage, and the beam current. Figure 245 shows the Probe control open.
Figure 245. Probe Control
There are two GFIS column probe tables; one for helium and one for neon. Access the 
probe table by clicking the arrow to open the drop-down menu. See Figure 246.
Note:
If you have the Capella option, there is an additional Ga FIB column probe 
table, which you would access through the Ga FIB Column tool panel.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 182](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-182.webp)

*Page 182 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

8.3
Imaging Mode Control
There are two ways to select the imaging mode. One way is to select the imaging 
mode is through the GFIS Column tool panel, in the Basic section. The current mode 
is displayed to the right of the words Imaging Mode. Click the arrow to the left of 
Imaging Mode and the button for changing the imaging mode appears. If the current 
imaging mode is Normal Imaging, the button is labeled View Source. Clicking it 
switches to SFIM mode, which is used to view the source. 
Figure 247. Current Mode: Normal Imaging
If the current mode is SFIM, the button is labeled View Sample. Clicking it switches to 
Normal Imaging, which is used to view the sample.
  
Figure 248. Current Mode: SFIM View
Another way is to use the Mode button on the knob panel. Each time this button is 
pressed the imaging mode toggles between Normal Imaging and SFIM.
The current imaging mode is always displayed in the bottom right corner of the UI.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 183](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-183.webp)

FOV (Field of View) Control
The FOV control is available only when Normal Imaging is selected as the imaging 
mode. For a description of FOV see Section 11.6, Field of View.
Figure 250. FOV Control 
Figure 251 shows the active FOV control. The current FOV is displayed to the right of 
FOV. The FOV control is linked directly to the Magnification knob on the knob panel. 
Moving the slider in this control has the same effect as turning the Magnification knob 
on the knob panel.
Figure 251. FOV Control Slider

SFIM FOV is determined by the magnitude of the deflection signals on the 
quadrupoles. The SFIM FOV control is displayed only when the imaging mode 
selected is SFIM mode as shown in Figure 252.
Figure 252. SFIM FOV Control

The image below shows the active SFIM FOV control. The current SFIM FOV is 
displayed to the right of SFIM FOV. The SFIM FOV is linked directly to the 
Magnification knob on the knob panel. Moving the slider in this control has the same 
effect as turning the Magnification knob on the knob panel.
Figure 253. SFIM FOV Control Slider
8.6
Working Distance Control
Working distance, the distance between the bottom of lens 2 to the sample when 
focused, is displayed in the right corner of the Working Distance control in Figure 254. It 
is not measured, but is calculated by the system based on the lens 2 voltage required 
to focus the beam on the sample. Therefore, the displayed working distance is only 
accurate when the image is in focus, See Section 11.10, Working Distance, for a graphic 
view of working distance. 
The slider in the Working Distance control is directly linked to lens 2. Moving the slider 
does not change the true working distance, but affects the focus. Moving this slider 
has the same effect as turning the Focus knob on the knob panel, which is to vary the 
lens 2 voltage.
Figure 254. Working Distance Control
8.7
Pan Control
Pan refers to moving the beam in the X and/or Y direction, which is reflected in the 
movement of the image. The Pan control is used when very small (<~10 µm) 
movement on the sample is required. For moving on the sample in such small 
magnitudes, it is easier to electronically move the beam rather than making 
mechanical movements with the stage. Generally, when panning, it is easier to use the 
Pan knobs on the knob panel than to use the control in the UI. See Section 3.10, 
Operator Console for a description of the Pan knobs. The UI pan control is shown in 
Figure 255.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 185](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-185.webp)

The step controls (the two concentric squares) in the above control are a bit awkward 
as the step controls do not increment by round numbers, but increment by 0.055 µm 
for the small controls, and 0.55 µm for the large controls. These may be cleared up in 
a future release of the software. If you want to try the step controls, the X and Y step 
controls are the inner and outer boxes. The inner boxes pan by 0.055 µm increments 
and the large boxes pan by 0.55 µm increments in each of the four directions (+ and - 
for X and Y).
Figure 256. Pan X & Y Controls
In the center of the control is a virtual joystick. This virtual joystick can be dragged in 
any direction to pan the beam. Dragging the joystick to the right has the same effect as 
turning the X pan knob in the clockwise direction. Dragging the joystick up has the 
same effect as turning the Y pan knob clockwise.
A useful feature of the virtual joystick is the ability to zero the X and Y pans. Right-click 
on the joystick to open a drop-down menu. See Figure 257. Some calibrations require 
that both X and Y pan are zero. Clicking on Zero X & Y will accomplish that.

8.8
Scan Rotate Control
The Scan Rotate control rotates the scanning raster. 
Figure 258. Scan Rotate Control
There are applications (beyond the scope of this manual) where you may want to 
change the direction and angle at which the beam passes over the sample. Under 
normal operation the scan rotation is 0 degrees. The current angle of the scan rotation 
is displayed to the right of Scan Rotate. The range of values displayed is from 180o to 
-180o. Rotating counter-clockwise is the positive direction; clockwise is negative.
To rotate the scan using the control in the UI, drag the green arrow in the direction of 
the desired rotation (Figure 258 and Figure 259).
Figure 259. Scan Rotate Virtual Joystick
Clicking in a quadrant causes the scan to jump to the boundary of that quadrant 
counter-clockwise.

gun is shown at the bottom of the control.

Figure 260. Gas Control
To start the flow of one of the gases, use the radio buttons to select the desired gas. In 
Figure 261, Helium has been selected. Once a gas has been selected, two more data 
items are displayed in the top line: the gas selected, and whether the gas flow is being 
regulated or not. The software regulates the pressure in the gun to the value selected 
(referred to as the gas set point), displayed to the right of the gas name (see Figure 
261).
Figure 261. Setting Gas Set Point
You can change the set point in a number of ways. One is to use the drop-down menu, 
as shown in Figure 262.
Figure 262. Gas Set Point Selection
When the drop-down menu is opened, a number of standard values are displayed. 
Select the set point you want.
Another way to change the set point is to use the arrows on either side of the displayed 
value. Each time the arrow on the right is clicked the set point is increased by 10%. 
Clicking the left arrow decreases the set point by 10%.
The set point can also be changed through the Gas Box tab in the Vacuum 
Dashboard. See Section Figure 202, . Pump Turn On/Off Popup in the Vacuum Dashboard 
section of this manual.

8.10
Landing Energy Control
Landing energy is the energy at which ions hit the sample, which is determined by the 
acceleration voltage applied to the source. The Landing Energy control is shown in 
Figure 263.
Figure 263. Landing Energy Control
The accelerator power supply output is displayed to the right of Landing Energy. Below 
Landing Energy is displayed the current saved value for BIV (Best Imaging Voltage) of 
the helium beam. To the right of that are drop-down menus for both the accelerator 
voltage and the extraction field.
The drop-down menu for accelerator voltage has a number of preselected values 
(Figure 264). There are some limits on the available acceleration voltages displayed. 
The accelerator can never be set higher in magnitude than the extraction field and can 
never be more than 25 kV below the extraction field. The accelerator voltage can also 
be set using the Accelerator control (see Section 8.14, Accelerator Control).
Figure 264. Selecting Accelerator Voltage
The Extractor control is enabled only when operating in SFIM mode; otherwise it is 
grayed out. There are three options; all based on the saved value of BIV for helium. 
When running helium the software automatically selects 100% of BIV. When running 
neon, the software selects 82% of BIV, because BIV for neon is typically about 82% of 
the value of BIV for helium. The last option, 105% of BIV, is selected by the software 
when the system is put into standby mode, on the principle that a higher accelerator 
voltage helps to keep the source free of contaminates.
Figure 265. Selecting Extractor Voltage

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 189](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-189.webp)

*Page 189 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

8.11
Aperture Control
The Aperture control, shown in Figure 266, displays the beam current, the aperture 
selected, and whether the aperture bar has been homed or not. If the aperture bar has 
not been homed, Not Homed is displayed at the top right corner of the control. In that 
case, the aperture bar must be homed before an aperture can be selected (see 
Section 5.3.1, Homing the Aperture Bar. See also Section 5.3.2, Selecting an Aperture.
Figure 266. Aperture Control
Clicking the alignment tool button, shown in the red box in the bottom right of the 
Aperture control, opens the GFIS Aperture Alignment tool panel (Figure 267). See 
Section 5.3, Aperture Selection and Alignment for information on the use of this window.
Figure 267. GFIS Aperture Alignment Control Panel
In the left center is the aperture alignment virtual joystick (Figure 268). Each pair of 
arrows has a small arrow and a large arrow, which are linked to the two fields below 
the joystick. Clicking a small arrow moves the aperture a small step; clicking a large 
arrow moves it a large step. You can set the sizes of the small and large steps. The 
default value for the small step is 1.00 µm; the large step is 10.00 µm. The directions 
shown on the graphic show which way the image moves on the screen when you click 
the arrows.

Figure 269. Spot Control

8.13
Stigmation
Stigmation control is used to correct for distortion in the shape of the ion beam. See 
Section11.4, Astigmatism for an explanation of astigmatism. By changing the stigmation 
values, you adjust the small DC voltages on the eight upper octopole plates. 
Stigmation can be controlled using either the Stigmator X and Y knobs on the knob 
panel or Stigmation control, shown below, in the GFIS Column tool panel. Generally, it 
is easier to use the knob panel controls. See Section 5.4.2, Stigmating the Beam for the 
procedure for stigmating the beam.

8.14
Accelerator Control
The Accelerator control allows you to set the acceleration voltage to any value, within 
a couple of constraints: the accelerator voltage can never be set to a higher magnitude 
than the extraction field voltage, and it can never be set to less than 25 kV of the 
extraction field voltage. The software prevents the setting of the acceleration voltage 
outside of these limits.
Figure 272. Accelerator Control

8.15
Extractor Control
The Extractor control allows you to set the extraction field voltage to any value, within 
a couple of constraints: the extractor value can only be changed when in the SFIM 
imaging mode (when operating in Normal Imaging mode this control is grayed out) and 
the extractor field voltage can never be set to a lower magnitude than the accelerator 
voltage, or more than 25 kV greater than the accelerator voltage. The software 
prevents the setting of the extractor field voltage outside of these limits.
Figure 273. Extractor Control
8.16
Lens 1 Control
The Lens 1 control is shown below. Use this to set lens 1 voltage to any desired value. 
Lens 1 voltage is linked directly to spot control. Typically, the lens 1 control is used only 
in SFIM imaging mode and Spot control is used in Normal Imaging mode. In SFIM 
mode, lens 1 is used to affect source magnification.
Figure 274. Lens 1 Control
8.17
Lens 2 Control
The Lens 2 control is shown below. Use this control to set lens 2 to any desired value. 
Lens 2 voltage is the focusing control and is linked directly to the Focus knob on the 
knob panel. Although the lens 2 control can be used to focus the beam on the sample, 
it is usually much easier to use the Focus knob.
Figure 275. Lens 2 Control

8.18
Beam Shift Control
Use the Beam Shift control as well as the Beam Tilt control to electronically fine-adjust 
small DC voltages on the upper and lower quadrupoles, which aligns the ion beam 
down the optical axis of the column. See the section, Quadrupoles or more information 
about the quadrupoles. See Section 5.3.3, Electronic Tilt and Shift Alignment for more 
information.
Use beam shift adjustments to fine-adjust wobble, typically in a FOV less than 5 µm.
Usually, it is much easier to use the Beam Shift knob on the knob panel, although this 
control can be used.
Note:
Make sure that the Tilt/Shift mode toggle is set for shift and not tilt (which 
displays on the bottom right of the UI).
Figure 276. Beam Shift Control
Use the control shown on the right in Figure 276 to shift the beam. Drag the horizontal 
green line to shift the beam in the Y direction or drag the vertical green line to shift the 
beam in the X direction. Use the blue dot on the control to shift the beam in both X and 
Y at the same time.
Use the text boxes on the left to enter beam shift values directly. This is most useful 
when setting these values to zero, as some calibrations require these values to be at 
zero.
8.19
Beam Tilt Control
Use the Beam Tilt control as well as the Beam Shift control to electronically fine-adjust 
small DC voltages on the upper and lower quadrupoles, which aligns the ion beam 
down the optical axis of the column. See the section, Quadrupoles for more information 
about the quadrupoles. See Section 5.3.3, Electronic Tilt and Shift Alignment for a 
description of using the Beam Shift control for aperture alignment.
Use beam tilt adjustments to fine-adjust current emission and image intensity.
Usually, it is much easier to use the Beam Tilt knob on the knob panel, although this 
control can be used.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 194](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-194.webp)

tilt. Drag the horizontal green line to adjust the tilt in the Y direction or drag the vertical 
green line to adjust the tilt in the X direction. Use the blue dot on the control to tilt in 
both X and Y at the same time.
Use the text boxes on the left to enter beam tilt values in directly. This is most useful

8.20
Column States Control
There are three separate column state controls within the Column States control.
Figure 278. Isolation Valve Control
8.20.1
Isolation Valve Control
You can control the column isolation valve (CIV) here as well as through the Vacuum 
Dashboard. Use the button at the top right, across from Isolation Valve is, to toggle the 
state of the CIV between Open or Close. The current state of the CIV is displayed just 
to the left of the button in Figure 278.
8.20.2
Scan Control Mode
Scan Control Mode is usually set to Internal, meaning that the SAS Module is driving 
the raster signals. The other two states are used when the raster is controlled by an 
external (to the NanoFab) scan generator, such as the NPVE option (Figure 279). The 
choice between ExternalActiveHigh and ExternalActiveLow is based on the logic level 
of the blanking signal from the external scan generator.

8.20.3
Blanking Mode
The Blanking Mode is normally left in Autoblanking. In this state the scan generator 
software determines when to blank and unblank the beam. The beam is blanked 
anytime the beam is not being scanned, and during retrace of the beam.
The Blanked and Unblanked states (Figure 280) are useful for troubleshooting purposes 
and are discussed in the troubleshooting section.

9
Presets and Probe Tables
9.1
Using Machine Presets
A preset is a way to use one mouse click to change the NanoFab settings to a 
frequently-used group of settings that would otherwise require several separate 
mouse clicks. Executing a preset changes several of the NanoFab’s settings in a 
logical sequence to arrive at a configuration useful for a specific purpose.
Presets can be used in combination to toggle between two frequently-used 
configurations. One example is imaging/scanning parameters. Imaging parameters 
include scan size (pixel resolution – 256 x 256 to 2048 x 2048), dwell time, type of 
averaging and number of averages.
It is common to use one set of these parameters to “tune” the beam (de-wobble, focus, 
stigmate). This function is usually done at some relatively fast scan rate, which is 
determined by the specific combination of imaging parameters.
It is also common to use a different set of parameters to capture a high quality image 
to save for presentation or further analysis. This function typically calls for higher pixel 
resolution and either longer dwell times or high averaging or some combination of 
both.
Presets can be created for each function (beam tuning and high quality imaging). 
When you want to capture a high quality image on a particular sample, click one preset 
to set the NanoFab parameters for beam tuning. When you are done tuning the beam, 
click another preset to set the NanoFab parameters for a high quality image. You can 
then simply click the Grab button to acquire an image for saving. This sequence can 
be repeated for new samples.
Another example for using a preset is with the flood gun (used for charge 
neutralization). Effective use of the flood gun requires adjustments to voltages that can 
be sample-dependent.  When a sample is unloaded, the flood gun must be powered 
down to protect it from relatively high pressure.  If, and when, a sample is loaded again 
and the flood gun is powered up, default voltages are put in place and not the specific 
voltages that may have been optimized for the sample.  If those voltages are stored in 
a preset, they can be restored easily by applying the preset after reloading the sample.
Following is a description of how you would set up presets for beam tuning and high 
resolution imaging, as an example of how two presets can work together to simplify 
your work flow.
Without a preset, each time you wanted to do high resolution imaging, you would first 
tune your beam as follows, in the Scan Parameters tool panel from the Acquisition 
panel
1.
Click the Acquisition tab in the upper left corner of the Zen UI to access the 
Acquisition tool panel and select Scan Parameters in the Acquisition panel to 
tune the beam up in the Scan Parameters tool panel.

9.2
Probe Tables
The Damascus software release introduced a new feature into the tool set of the 
NanoFab: the probe table. Probe tables have been a staple of the gallium column in 
SmartSEM, and we have recognized the utility of the feature and chose to integrate it. 
With this new feature come new user interfaces and system behavior, which this 
section addresses.
9.2.1
Probe and Probe Table Basics
A probe is a set of column settings that configure the column for a specific beam 
current, landing energy, and beam size for the performance of a particular task. The 
parameter settings are listed in a probe table, and you give it a name that is 
recognizable by you for that task. As you develop other tasks that have repetitive 
settings and create probes for them, you add them to your table. For each probe you 
assign a particular value to each of the parameters listed in the table above, for the 
type of column/beam type you are using.
Examples: Helium High Resolution; Neon milling 
Note:
Use a probe instead of selecting an aperture and other settings.
The idea of the probe table is to use a probe (or “beam”) for a specific task rather than 
selecting an aperture and making other adjustments and settings for that specific task. 
You can incorporate the appropriate aperture in the probe but also include other 
settings appropriate for that task (accelerating voltage, spot control/beam current, 
electronic beam alignment settings specific to the spot control setting, etc.)
For example, you could create a probe for high resolution imaging. This probe could 
incorporate a small aperture, high spot control setting, high accelerating voltage, 
electronic beam tilt specific to the spot control setting and settings for the x and y 
stigmators. By selecting the probe, all of these settings are put into place.
As another example, you can create a probe for patterning in a gold sample. Perhaps 
this patterning requires a relatively high beam current. You may incorporate a relatively 
large aperture and/or low spot control setting in the probe for this task, and additional 
settings specific to that aperture and spot control. These settings are different than 
those used for high resolution imaging.
In this scenario, you can select the “Gold Patterning” probe, use the probe to make a 
pattern, and then select the “High Res Imaging” probe to take an image of the pattern. 
The system would automatically change aperture, spot control, alignment, stigmator 
and other settings as appropriate.
You can adjust or maintain each probe by selecting the probe, making necessary 
adjustments (to the stigmator values, for example) and saving the changes (see 
below).
New probes can be created for new tasks as needed by adding an entry from the 
probe table.
Once you have probes listed in your tables, you can then apply them by the press of a 
button. Upon being applied, each setting in the selected probe is written to the column,

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 207](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-207.webp)

*Page 207 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

360800-0000-024 Rev 2
