which makes the necessary changes and reports back status messages. Each probe 
table has a Reference Probe. Delta parameters in the table use the reference probe 
and the previous probe to determine the new value (see Figure 301).
Note:
If no probe is selected, the reference probe is selected at power up.
Figure 301. A Probe Table

Although this table is labeled GFIB Helium Probe Table, the table is a 
GFIS table, not a Ga FIB table. It is named incorrectly. 
There are two probe tables for the GFIS column: one for the helium beam, and another 
for the neon beam. The Capella column has one probe table for the gallium beam.
Note:
“Spot Number” in the GFIS lists of parameters refers to “Spot Control”.

9.2.2
Column User Interfaces
The user interfaces for the GFIS and Gallium columns have been simplified in an effort 
to give the user the most useful information in the simplest way possible. The goal of 
this redesign was to provide all of the controls needed to run each column, assuming 
normal operating conditions, without any redundant controls.
Figure 303. GFIS Column Tool Panel
This simplicity is achieved by the versatility of probes. Probes combine Landing 
Energy, Aperture position, Beam Current, and various other settings which affect the 
characteristics of the beam, so many previously unrelated controls can now be hidden, 
while still providing the user with full control of the column.
All of the settings governed by the probe control are still available by clicking the 
“Show All” option in the upper right corner.
Figure 304. Probe Tool Panel
The probe control allows users to select and apply probes, and access the table(s) for 
reference or advanced editing. To apply a probe, simply select the probe wanted from 
the drop-down list, and click the Apply Probe button next to it. 
As the GFIS column has a probe table for each gas, there are 2 sets of probe table 
controls. If the user applies a probe associated with the gas that is not currently 
flowing, the system automatically takes the appropriate actions of switching gasses. 
The sprocket button to the right of the Apply Probe button opens the associated probe 
table (Figure 304).
9.2.3
Probe Editing
Because probes encompass a wide range of features that must all be set together, 
there is now a Probe Edit mode for each column, accessible via the Mode drop-down 
menu at the top of the column page (Figure 305).

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 209](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-209.webp)

*Page 209 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 210

| 205 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Figure 305. Mode Drop-down menu
The Probe Edit tool panel provides all the functionality necessary to tune and define 
the beam, with no need to go back and forth to the Normal Mode tool panel under 
normal operating conditions.
Figure 306. Probe Edit Control Panel
Changes made on this panel affect the column in real time, but are not committed to 
the table until you click the Save button in the top right corner. If you want to revert the 
column to the last saved settings, click the Undo button to reapply the current probe, 
overwriting any unsaved changes.
The Probe Edit tool panel also provides controls to change Pan, Working Distance and 
Field of View. While these settings are not saved as part of the probe, they are useful 
when tuning the beam, thus justifying their inclusion on the panel.
You may find it more convenient to use the normal UI with knobs to tweak everything 
(SFIM alignment with MC Tilt, L2 wobble with MC Shift, focus, stigmation), and then go 
to the Probe Edit tool panel, click the Save button, and then back to Normal mode. The

Note:
The Aperture Offset section of the Probe Edit tool panel should only be 
used for the Ga FIB column.
9.2.4
Patterning
Probes were originally created to improve the consistency and to reduce the overhead 
of patterning, so the probes are tightly integrated into the Zen patterning engine. Each 
pattern may specify the probe which is used to mill it. 
Each column’s probes feature a Mill Defocus parameter. This value is used to apply an 
offset to the focus voltage while patterning, and is a useful tool for optimizing pattern 
milling and deposition, especially with the larger beam currents of the Ga FIB column.
In the context of patterning, probes may be used in conjunction with machine presets 
(see Section 9.1, Using Machine Presets). In this case, the probe is applied first, followed 
by the machine preset. As machine presets can overwrite the values set by probes, 
care should be taken to ensure that the combination of probe and presets set all of, 
and only, the parameters necessary to perform the pattern operation, or unintended 
behavior may ensue.
Figure 307. Shape Properties Tool Panel - Apply Probe selected

9.2.5
Automation
The probe tables are integrated into the NanoFab automation software as well. 
Developers are able to get information about each column’s probe table(s), apply 
probes, and even create new probes from within their work flows.
Figure 308. Lists of Probe Tables
The ProbeTableInfo activities in the Properties panel (Figure 309) allow the user to
–
Query and get probes by name
–
Obtain a collection of all probes in the table
–
Obtain the reference probe
–
Obtain the selected probe
–
Create and get a new probe
Figure 309. Probe Properties Panel
The probe objects returned by the ProbeTableInfo activities expose the settings of the 
probe, which are both readable and writable. Writing a new value to a setting this way 
will immediately save it.
Probes can be added to patterns created via automation as well. This can be done by 
assigning a probe to the Probe field in the CreateScanParameters activity.

9.2.6
Probe Table Display
Each probe table may be viewed in its entirety by going to the Probe control on the 
column user interface panel and clicking the sprocket icon that corresponds with the 
desired probe table. Probes may be created or deleted from the probe table display 
window using the + and Garbage Can buttons in the lower right corner. Changes made 
to the probe table from this window are immediately saved. Any probe can be applied 
from this window by right-clicking on the row for that probe and clicking Apply Probe 
from the context menu.
The probe table display window is intended for probe management by tool 
administrators, and should be used with care to avoid accidental changes or deletions 
of probes.
Note:
There are currently software issues with editing the text in these cells: see 
the Note at the bottom of the next page.

Putting Probes to Work

To use a probe:

Expand the Probe section of the GFIS Column tool panel.
•
Select the probe from the Probe drop-down list.
•
Click the Apply Probe button.
Adjusting Probes
To adjust a probe:

With the appropriate probe applied, make adjustments (accelerating voltage, spot 
control, electronic beam tilt and shift, stigmators, focus, etc.).

Creating New Probes
To create a new probe:
•
Make all the necessary changes to the system for the probe you want to create 
(accelerating voltage, aperture size, spot control, electronic beam tilt, electronic 
beam shift, focus, stigmation, etc.).
•
Expand the Probe section of the GFIS Column tool panel.
•
Select the cog icon to the right of the Apply Probe button.
•
Click the + icon in the lower right corner of the probe table
•
Highlight the new probe name in the left-most column of the probe table and cre-
ate an appropriate name
•
Close the probe table by clicking the X in the upper left corner (this saves the new 
probe – there is no Save button in the probe table.
•
To use this probe immediately, select it from the Probe drop-down list and click the 
Apply Probe button (otherwise, the probe that was in place just prior to creating 
the new one will still be displayed).
Since the Save button in Probe Edit mode is not used in this sequence, the probe 
that was in place just prior to the new probe being created is not disrupted.
Note:
It is recommended that you create new probes using this sequence, and 
not by entering condition values in the Selected Probe table screen, due 
to some anomalies in the Damascus/Delft releases of the software that 
will be resolved in a future release; namely
–
The probe tables do not specify what units are being used for any of the 
conditions; for instance, beam current may be seen as pAs in the UI, but 
appear as Amps in the probe table, so 5 pA would appear as 5......E-12.
–
Entering decimal points: Decimal points can not be entered after any 
numerical value; therefore, if you enter 0.5, it appears as 5, or if you enter 
5.3, it appears as 53.
–
Apertures: Apertures are numbered from 1 to 15 in all other screens in the 
UI, but are numbered from 0 to 14 in the probe tables.
–
If you enter a value into the probe table with, for instance, two decimal 
places, and save it in the Probe Edit screen, it is converted to 14 decimal 
places and thus can be somewhat unrecognizable.

10
Correlative Microscopy
10.1
Overview
Shuttle & Find is a UI module that allows users to locate and examine image features 
on one enabled Zeiss product, e.g., light microscope, Crossbeam system, and the 
NanoFab, and transfer those images to other enabled Zeiss products so that sample 
positions of the images can be precisely located and examined in the receiving tool. 
Users can now transfer features of interest back and forth between any products that 
support Shuttle & Find.
The Shuttle & Find module provides a user interface and data management which 
essentially stores a series of lock points in two coordinate spaces. These points are 
used to reference features in either coordinate space. Three lock points are selected 
as this gives the space scale in both X and Y as well as rotation and orthogonality, as 
shown in the diagram below.
10.1.1
A Summary of the Process
1.
A sample of interest is loaded into the microscope and three lock points are 
located using the Shuttle & Find module, which establishes a coordinate space 
that the features of interest will be referenced in, called the original coordinate 
space.
2.
The locations of any number of features of interest are found, which are automati-
cally stored in the Shuttle & Find coordinate space when the image is saved in 
Zen using the .CZI image format.
3.
The holder with the sample is loaded into another (or even the same) microscope 
and the same three lock points are located, which establish a working coordinate

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 215](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-215.webp)

*Page 215 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 216

| 211 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
space and a lock between the two coordinate systems whereby all features of 
interest from the original coordinate space can be located in the working coordi-
nate space. 
4.
The images from the original microscope are loaded into the working microscope. 
By matching the reference locations in the original images to those in the live 
image, Shuttle & Find allows you to locate all features of interest easily, even if the 
working coordinate space has a different scale and rotation.
5.
The image sizes and orientations in the two coordinate spaces are matched.
10.2
Zeiss Shuttle & Find Holders
Zeiss provides standard holders for use in Shuttle & Find that fit into the standard 
Zeiss dovetail. The Orion NanoFab can accept any of these holders.
Other holders can be used for correlative microscopy. Users can lock any two 
coordinate spaces by finding the matching lock points on any holder that has features 
of interest relative to the same coordinate space. The standard Shuttle & Find holders 
have the advantage of having built-in lock points which appear in the Sample 
Management tool panels. All holders that are listed in the Sample Management tool 
panel have a wire frame outline that is used in other parts of the UI. Selecting a holder 
in the Sample Management tool panel updates the software everywhere the sample 
holder is needed.
If using other holders, there may be limitations on the distances you can use when 
creating lock points, due to differences in imaging systems. For example, the stage 
travel distance is 50 mm for the NanoFab, allowing a maximum travel with the Shuttle 
& Find holder of 43 mm. If the Original Coordinate Space is created with a distance 
between lock points greater than 43 mm, a NanoFab user would not be able to lock the 
two coordinate spaces. The NanoFab correlative holder has such lock points 
permanently located on the three tabs machined into the holder which are within the 
allowable travel range.

appears and Shuttle & Find is enabled.

The license is created

10.3.3
Establishing the Coordinate System
Use this section to establish mapping between original and coordinate working spaces 
in ZEN (Yellow Edition) on the Orion NanoFab. The process is the same, whether you 
are creating the original coordinate space or the working coordinate space.
Note:
If using the Zeiss correlative holder, choosing lock points is not an issue.  
The NanoFab correlative holder has such features permanently located 
on the three tabs machined into the holder. If using another holder, it is 
important to choose appropriate lock points on the sample. Using Shuttle 
& Find between systems having a wide dissimilarity in resolution can 
affect the ability to find features of interest when going from higher to 
lower resolution systems with feature sizes that are smaller than the 
working system. This typically induces small scale and rotational errors. 
Also, when choosing lock points, consider using locations that are as 
precise as possible, and which create a right-angled coordinate space. A

10.3.5
Transporting Holder Between Enabled Zeiss Systems
You can transport the sample in and amongst any Zeiss equipment that supports 
Shuttle & Find and can accept the correlative holder.
1.
Once mounted onto the target system holder, load the sample using the loading 
method of the target system and then locate the Shuttle & Find feature in the tar-
get system software. If the target system is a Zen implementation, the same func-
tionality as described here can be used. If the target system is a Zeiss product 
that uses SmartSEM, please refer to the SmartSEM user manual for activating 
Shuttle & Find. 
2.
Load a .CZI file with the coordinate marking positions. Then perform the same 
coordinate lock process as described above. Once completed the two coordinate 
systems are locked.

Not every microscope has the same magnification or field of view. To view

them so the features of interest appear in the same scale and orientation

you can find features in the original image and lock those to the working 
image.

that is used to do a secondary lock, image to image.
Locking the images follows the same process as locking the coordinate systems, with 
the same end result: two images that are of the same scale, rotation and orthogonality.

11
Some Imaging Concepts
11.1
The Trimer
The 3-atom cluster at the tip of the GFIS source is called the trimer. you forms a 3-
sided pyramid on the end of the tip, using a proprietary process called Form the 
Source, located in the GFIS Source Maintenance Wizard of the UI. Then, using Select 
a Trimer, you strips away atoms until only the 3-atom formation is left at the tip.
Figure 311. Formed Trimer Seen in SFIM mode
Eventually, one or more of the atoms that are part of the trimer break away from the tip. 
When this occurs the process of selecting a trimer must be repeated from the existing 
pyramid, or, if necessary, the pyramid must be rebuilt. 
The life time of a trimer is a result of a few factors, the most important being the type of 
gas the source is exposed to. A trimer that is exposed only to helium for imaging 
typically lasts a couple of weeks. A trimer that is mostly used for neon imaging might 
last for a much shorter period of time.
11.2
Best Imaging Voltage (BIV)
One of the key parameters when evaluating a source is its best imaging voltage (BIV). 
BIV refers to the extraction field setting that produces the highest rate of ionization at 
the trimer for that source. The BIV varies from source to source, and also changes for 
a given source as it ages. See Figure 312 for a typical BIV curve (trimer beam current 
as a function of extraction field.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 223](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-223.webp)

*Page 223 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 224

| 219 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Figure 312. BIV Curve
Determine BIV in the GFIS Source Maintenance Wizard of the UI is used to find the 
BIV for a source, while using helium as the imaging gas. The BIV for neon is about 82 
percent of the BIV for helium (discussed in more detail in the neon imaging section). 
The spec for helium BIV is 25kV to 35kV, although most operators prefer a BIV at the 
higher end of this range.
With a very low extraction field, there is no beam current (see Figure 312). There is 
usually no ionization, hence no ion beam, until the extraction field reaches between 
20kV and 24kV. The point at which the ionization is first observed is referred to as “first 
light”. After that point, the ionization rate increases as the extraction field is increased. 
At a certain point, however, the ionization rate at the trimer begins to decrease as the 
extraction field is increased further. Protruding atoms on the source at a distance from 
the trimer begin to ionize helium, reducing the available helium at the trimer. 
The extraction field setting that gives the maximum trimer beam current is the BIV. 
Setting the extraction field well beyond BIV can pull the trimer off of the source, and 
may even cause permanent damage to the source.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 224](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-224.webp)

*Page 224 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 220 |
11.3
Focus
The focus is the point where all beam ions converge in a small spot. This is controlled 
by the objective lens (lens 2). When the focus is adjusted, the focus point is moved up 
and down the optical axis. The focus is set correctly when the focus point is directly on 
the sample surface, resulting in a sharp image. If the focus is above or below the 
sample, the image is out of focus and appears blurry.
Figure 313. Focus
Figure 314. Focus Examples
Focus point above sample surface; 
the image is out of focus.
Focus is on the sample 
surface; image is in focus.
Focus is below sample surface; 
image is out of focus

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 225](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-225.webp)

*Page 225 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 226

| 221 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
11.4
Astigmatism
Ideally, the ion beam would be perfectly round when it strikes the sample. However, 
there are a number of things that can cause the ion beam to be somewhat elliptical in 
shape (Figure 315). When this type of distortion is caused by a lens it is called 
astigmatism. The term is used in this context as a general reference to a non-circular 
beam, regardless of the cause. A non-circular beam results in a distortion of the 
image. The distortion is most easily seen when sweeping the lens 2 focus control 
through the range of best focus. When out of focus, the image has a noticeable 
directional distortion. As the focus is adjusted from out of focus, through the focus 
point, and beyond to out of focus again, the direction of the distortion changes by 90 
degrees (Figure 315, right side). The ion beam is brought back into a circular shape by 
you, using the stigmator controls, as described in Section 5.3.5, Mechanical Aperture 
Alignment.
Figure 315. Astigmatism

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 226](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-226.webp)

*Page 226 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 227

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 222 |
11.5
Contrast and Brightness
The Contrast control adjusts the gain through the ET detector, which has the effect of 
increasing the difference between darker and lighter pixels, giving a wider overall 
range of intensity of the image pixels. The Brightness control simply adds a DC offset 
to all the pixels during processing of the signal from the ET detector.
Figure 316. Contrast and Brightness
Figure 317. Contrast and Brightness Controls
Brightness
Contrast

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 227](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-227.webp)

*Page 227 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 228

| 223 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
11.6
Field of View
Field of view (FOV) refers to the size of the area being scanned by the ion beam. In the 
NanoFab the FOV is always a square. For example, setting the image FOV to 1 µm 
means that the ion beam will scan an area of 1 µm by 1 µm, hence the displayed 
image represents an area of 1 µm by 1 µm. The largest FOV available depends on a 
number of factors, such as working distance and acceleration voltage. The smallest 
FOV available is 100 nm by 100 nm.
FOV is determined by the magnitude of the deflection signals in the octopoles.
Figure 318. Field of View
11.7
Scan Size
Scan size refers to the number of pixels per line (x-direction) and the number of lines 
(y-direction) per frame (one full scan of the field of view). There are four available 
sizes: 
•
256 x 256
•
512 x 512
•
1024 x 1024
•
2048 x 2048
Note that the scan size does not affect FOV. For a given FOV, selecting a higher scan 
size results in the pixels being closer together. In general, a larger scan size gives a 
higher resolution image for a given FOV.
10 µm FOV 
2 µm FOV

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 228](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-228.webp)

*Page 228 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 229

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 224 |
Figure 319. Resolution Examples
11.8
Dwell Time/Scan Speed
The dwell time sets the time in which the beam remains at a single scan point before 
moving to the next scan point. A longer dwell time gives you a slower scan speed and 
means that the beam stays longer at a single scan point, which can create more signal 
but do more damage to the sample. A shorter dwell time gives you a higher scan 
speed because the beam does not stay as long at a single scan point. The image 
appears more noisy as less signal is created in this shorter time.
If an image is noisy, increasing the dwell time improves the image quality by increasing 
the signal-to-noise ratio. If your sample is easily damaged, a quicker scan time in 
combination with averaging (see next section) might be the best option.
Figure 320. Dwell Time Examples

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 229](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-229.webp)

*Page 229 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 230

| 225 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
11.9
Averaging
Averaging can be used to improve the image quality and is used in combination with 
shorter dwell times. Averaging is often used for beam-sensitive or charging samples.
Off (no averaging): When recording the image, the area is scanned point by point and 
line by line until the final scan point is reached. This setting is used for all alignments 
and for recording most images.
Line: When recording the image, the first line is scanned a selected number of times 
the signal for each pixel is averaged. Once finished the next line is scanned the 
selected number of times and the signal averaged. This process is repeated until the 
last line is scanned.
Frame: When recording the image, the entire image is scanned a selected number of 
times N and the signal averaged.
11.10
Working Distance
The working distance is the distance between the surface of the sample and the 
bottom of lens 2. The current working distance is calculated by the system based on 
the lens 2 voltage required to focus the beam on the sample. Therefore, the working 
distance calculation is only accurate when the image is in focus.
Figure 321. Working Distance
Working Distance

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 230](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-230.webp)

*Page 230 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 231

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 226 |
11.11
Eucentric Height
Eucentric height is the working distance at which the sample is exactly on the tilt axis. 
When the sample is at eucentric height, there is no displacement of the displayed 
image and no focus change as the stage is tilted. Instead of eucentric height, it might 
be better to think of it as “eucentric working distance”.
11.12
Charge Neutralization
Charging of the sample can occur with insulating (non-conductive) or semi-conductive 
materials. The positive charge which is introduced in the sample by the ion beam is not 
conducted away and builds up. The signal (secondary electrons) is negatively charged 
and is attracted back to the positively charged sample instead of reaching the detector. 
Charging is therefore represented by black areas (or images which get darker with 
every scan). To reduce this charging effect, the flood gun can be used to direct a beam 
of electrons to the area where the ion beam is scanning. The electrons replace those 
displaced by the ion beam, thus neutralizing the charging effect on the sample.
Figure 322. Charge Neutralization

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 231](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-231.webp)

*Page 231 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 232

| 227 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
11.13
Crossover
The expressions “above crossover”, “below crossover” and “at crossover” are used in 
various places in this manual, particularly when discussing the alignment of the tilt of 
the gun head. These expressions are used idiomatically, and therefore require some 
explanation to understand their use.
As the beam leaves the source it tends to diverge, creating a cone-shaped beam with 
the apex at the source. As the beam passes through lens 1 (also known as the 
condenser lens) the beam is pushed back towards a single point, much as would be 
done to focus the beam. The point at which th beam converges is called the crossover 
point. After the crossover point, the beam begins to diverge again.
Figure 323. Above Crossover
The magnitude of the lens 1 voltage determines where the crossover point is. The 
higher the lens 1 voltage, the higher up the column the crossover point is. When 
aligning the mechanical tilt of the gun head it is necessary to move the crossover point 
above and below the beam limiting aperture. (During gun tilt alignment the beam 
limiting apertures are moved out of the beam path.) When the crossover point is 
positioned below the beam limiting aperture, we refer to it as “below crossover”. When 
the crossover point is positioned right at the beam limiting aperture, we refer to that as 
“at crossover”. Above the beam limiting aperture, it is referred to as “above crossover”.
Generally, crossover is only of concern when operating in SFIM mode and observing 
the trimer. When looking at a given trimer above and below crossover, the trimer when 
observed below crossover will be the mirror image, in both axes, of what was seen 
above crossover. In looking at Figure 323 and Figure 325, notice that the brightest atom 
is in the lower left of the cluster when above crossover but when observed at below 
crossover, that brightest atom is now in the upper left of the cluster.
When at crossover, the SFIM image is a bright circle (Figure 324). That is because the 
beam is in focus right at the beam limiting aperture.

11.14
Spot Control
Spot control is a gradient scale from 1 to 10 that represents the beam crossover 
position in the column. Spot 1 puts the crossover at the beam limiting aperture. At spot 
1 all of the beam current passes through the aperture and hits the sample, giving a 
maximum beam current at the expense of resolution. 
When you raise the spot number you increase lens 1 voltage, moving the crossover 
higher in the column, which results in a wider beam at the aperture. Therefore some of 
the beam is blocked by the aperture, resulting in a lower beam current, but a higher 
resolution. 
A lower spot raises source magnification. At spot 1 the source is magnified and so is 
the vibration, giving a large beam diameter and the greatest amplification of any 
source vibrations.
If at spot 1 the aperture is not in focus, recalibrate the source distance (see Section 
5.2.7, Set Source Distance).
Note:
Avoid prolonged use at spot 1 with the gold apertures, as that can damage 
the apertures. Use moly apertures when available.
Figure 326. Spot 1
Crossover

12
Patterning
ORION NanoFab features an integrated hardware and software control system: the 
NanoPatterning and Visualization Engine (NPVE), which incorporates a dedicated 16-
bit scan generator for each NanoFab column and dual-signal acquisition hardware 
supporting real-time advanced patterning and visualization.
You are able to completely control the beam with the use of a GUI that allows you to 
create a range of fully edible shapes including rectangles, trapezoids, polygons, lines, 
polylines, ellipses and spots. You can then vector-fill these shapes while maintaining 
full control over dose variation and patterning parameters including patterning 
direction, spacing, dwell, refresh and scan strategy.
12.1
Patterning Basics
Patterning is the process of removing sample atoms in specific areas on the sample 
with the ion beam. During patterning the ion beam is vector-scanned across the 
sample surface. The beam stays in a single scan point for a designated time (dwell 
time) before it moves to the next scan point. While milling, the ion beam overlaps to 
ensure a smooth edge cutting. Multiple patterns can be milled at the same time and 
complex patterns created from the basic geometric figures. The features that can be 
created are only limited by your imagination! 
Patterning can also be an additive process when the ion beam is used to deposit 
material onto the sample surface using a Gas Injection System (GIS). The GIS flows a 
precursor gas onto the sample surface and the ion beam reacts chemically with the 
precursor to deposit material only where the beam is directed.
It is essential that the ion beam is well aligned when patterning. An astigmatic or 
unfocused beam leads to larger or distorted figures and different milling depths.
Figure 328. Beam Misalignment
When patterning, the ion beam stays in a single scan point and removes sample 
atoms. The ion beam is then moved to the next scan point. The ion beam usually 
overlaps 50% between adjacent scan points to create smooth edges (See Figure 329).

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 237](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-237.webp)

*Page 237 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

12.1.1
Patterning Parameters
Acceleration Voltage (Ion Beam Energy) 
Acceleration voltage, ion species, and material density determines how deep the ions 
travel into the sample and determines the resolution.
High Acceleration Voltage - At higher energy, ions travel further into the sample. 
SRIM simulations can be used to determine the depth to which the ions travel. He 
ions can travel a few hundred nm deep into the sample, Ne less than 100 nm, and 
Ga less deep.
Lower Acceleration Voltage - At lower energy, ions travel less distance into the 
sample.
Figure 330. SRIM Simulation: Ion Range - He, Ne, Ga
Note:
Ref: “Stopping and range of ions in matter”, version SRIM 2013.00, 
www.SRIM.org

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 238](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-238.webp)

360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 234 |
Aperture and Spot Control Value Selection
The aperture determines the ion beam current as well as the beam diameter. Larger 
apertures provide more current for milling, the beam is larger. Smaller apertures 
provide less current but a smaller beam diameter, which means that smaller structures 
can be milled. Since milling depth is directly related to beam current, patterning with 
smaller apertures takes longer than patterning with larger apertures for a given pattern 
size. The appropriate aperture/spot control value depends on the sample, as well as 
the structure size which needs to be milled. Note that increasing the spot control value 
decreases beam size, and vice versa.
Dwell Time/Passes 
The dwell time determines how long the ion beam stays in a single point. The dwell 
time is directly linked to the amount of material which is removed in the milling 
process: longer dwell times remove more material. The dwell time is usually linked to 
the Passes parameter (Repeats in NPVE). 
Passes set the amount of repeats in which the ion beam is scanned over the 
patterning area. In most cases, a short dwell time, 1 µs, is used with several thousand 
passes to achieve the desired pattern depth (or dose). Alternatively, a single pass with 
a very long dwell time (ms) can be used. Both approaches have their advantages and 
disadvantages.
Beam Overlap 
The beam overlap determines how much the beam overlaps between the different 
scan points. It’s default value is 50%. The beam conventionally overlaps when milling 
in order to achieve a smooth edge. If the beams do not overlap, a rough edge is 
obtained. This is illustrated below. Beam overlaps are usually changed when the 
sample melts or phase transformations occur Figure 331).
Figure 331. Beam Overlap

The scan direction is used to control the direction of redeposition. Redeposition of the 
sputtered sample atoms occurs at the backside of the scan direction (where the scan 
started) (Figure 332). 
Figure 332. Scan Direction and Redeposition

Ion Dose (a.k.a Fluence) - The ion dose is 
the amount of ions which travel through a 
specific surface area on the sample:
–At low doses (< 5-10% implanted spe-
cies): individual disordered regions are cre-
ated within the sample.
–At high doses (>5-10% implanted spe-
cies): the individual regions can overlap 
and form an amorphous layer.
Figure 333. Dosage Effects
Volume Per Dose
Every material mills at different speeds. This means that the same ion dose mills to 
different depths in different materials. The parameter Volume per Dose describes how 
much material (how much volume) is removed for a specific dose (determined by the 
beam current and the time milled).
Dose Array -The dose array is used to determine the volume per dose for the element 
(which will then allow you to indirectly set the milling depth). You need your sample on 
top of a charging substrate (glass) for this experiment. Alternatively, you will need the 
FIB to determine the milling depth after your dose array. Another alternative is to 
measure the depths of the milled areas by tilting the sample and measuring from the 
sample surface to the bottom of the milled area. 
Note:
in this case, the milled area dimensions and the tilt angle must be 
appropriate so that the bottom of the milled area can be seen. An 
appropriate tilt correction factor must also be applied to the 
measurements.
The idea is to create an array of the same structure (line or rectangle) and mill the 
structure with different doses. If the sample thickness is known, you check out at which 
dose the sample was milled away to the charging substrate (which will appear black). 
This way, you know how deep the sample was milled (z-value) (see Figure 334). You

12.2
Patterning Basics Using NPVE
Patterning can be done with helium or neon. Use helium for features less than 200 nm, 
and Neon for features over 10 nm.
Note: Operating with neon requires a lower extractor voltage, which is automatically 
set by Zen when switching the gas. The theoretical BIV for neon is 82% of that for 
helium (based on first ionization energies).
Following are descriptions of some of the patterning concepts and processes. You can 
find much more information in the NPVE User Guide, found in Program Files (x86) > 
Fibics > NPVE 4Z > Documentation.
12.2.1
Patterning Process
The general process for patterning:
1.
Select the required aperture (refer to "Select Aperture" on page 137 and "Beam 
Limiting Apertures" on page 21).
2.
Select the required acceleration voltage.
3.
Align the HIM using helium gas.
4.
When aligned, switch to neon.
5.
Record an image of the sample in NPVE.
6.
Draw patterns.
7.
Set pattern parameters.
8.
Determine when to stop the pattern (after specified time, dose, or repeats, or by 
using endpoint images or graph) to determine patterning depth.
Note:
Use Dose Array to determine milling depth of sample (different for each 
material) if known.
9.
Press START.
10. Start the patterning process.
Note:
You can pause the pattern anytime if required.

Advanced Settings
–
Dwell time: sets the time that the ion beam stays in a single scan point
–
Spacer: can be set to either relative spacing (between scan points), rela-

12.2.4
Creating Multiple Patterns
You can draw multiple patterns in the Geometry window, and then move them around 
with the mouse. You build complex structures by placing different patterns over each 
other. The patterns can be milled serial (first pattern 1, then pattern two, etc.) or 
parallel (pattern 1 and pattern 2, etc.) at the same time.
–
serial patterning (default)): Mills pattern 1 first, and then mills the second 
pattern.
–
parallel patterning (select parallel patterns): Mills all patterns at the 
same time: (in parallel).
Note: Parallel milling means that pattern 1 is milled first to a specific 
depth, then pattern 2 is milled to the same depth, than pattern 1 is milled a 
bit more, and so forth. This is determined by repeats.
–
Void: Does not mill the drawn area. This can be used to created complex 
shapes. Two examples are given in the image below (middle and right 
images, Figure 340).
–
Outline: Mills the shape outline only. An example is given in the left image 
below, Figure 340).

13
Glossary
Accelerator - A positive voltage applied to the source, used to accelerate the ions to 
their final energy.
Adatoms – Contaminant atoms that attach to the source. A contraction of “adsorbed 
atoms”, which refers to the adhesion of atoms from a gas, liquid, or dissolved solid to 
a surface.
Aperture - A small opening in the beam path that forms and limits the electron or ion 
beam.
Astigmatism – Lens aberration that distorts the shape of the electron beam, 
compensated by the stigmator.
Bakeout – Degassing of surfaces of a vacuum system by heating during the pumping 
process.
Trimer - A group of three atoms that reside on the tip of the gas field ion source.
Bias voltage – The amount of voltage that an electronic device needs in order to 
power on and function.
Big Hole – When there is no limiting aperture bar in the beamline, the beam is focused 
on the blanking aperture which allows passage of a broad beam for the purpose of 
scanning by the quadrupoles in SFIM mode. The void created by the removal of the 
beam limiting aperture is idiomatically referred to as “big hole”.
BIV - Best Image Voltage - The extractor voltage that makes the brightest image when 
viewing the trimer in SFIM mode.
Blanking – Deflection of the beam so that it misses the orifice in the blanking aperture. 
When a particle beam column is powered up but not being used it is normally blanked. 
Deflection directs the beam into the aperture element, which absorbs them.
Blanking aperture – A disc with a relatively large central orifice downstream from the 
blanking plates (deflection electrodes) through which the beam passes when in an 
unblanked state. When DC voltages of opposite polarities are applied to the blanking 
plates, the beam particles are repelled by one plate and attracted by the other, the net 
effect being that the beam misses the orifice and is absorbed by the aperture element. 
Also known as “Big hole”.
Capella Column - The ORION NanoFab gallium focused ion beam column option
Characterization - The broad and general process by which a material's structure and 
properties are probed and measured.
CIV – Column Isolation Valve. Isolates column from chamber. It sits right above the 
aperture assembly.
Coincident Point - The point where both the Ga-FIB and GFIS beams are focused 
with the stage at eucentric position
Cryogenic - The study of the production and behavior of materials at very low 
temperatures (below −150 °C, −238 °F or 123 K)

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 249](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-249.webp)

*Page 249 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 250

| 245 |
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
5/29/18
Deflection – To bend the beam path through the application of positive (repelling) and 
negative (attracting) voltages on opposite sides of the beam
Degassing – The process of heating a gauge in order to remove unwanted or excess 
gas
Detector – A mechanism for detecting image signals consisting of secondary 
electrons or backscattered electrons, and generating a corresponding intensity value 
for each pixel of the scan.
Dwell Time - The amount of time the beam remains at a single scan point before 
moving to the next scan point. 
Eucentric Height - The working distance at which the sample is exactly on the tilt axis.
Eucentric Point – The point at which the tilt rotational axis meets the beam rotational 
axis, guaranteeing that the focus is maintained when the sample is tilted at a certain 
working distance.
Eucentric Position – Same as Eucentric Height.
Evactron - The plasma cleaner that eliminates hydrocarbon contamination by 
generating plasma inside the chamber.
Extractor – Biased electrode, which, by creating a potential difference with the 
source, creates a strong electrical field that attracts electrons from the gas atoms in 
the gas field and ionizes them.
Extraction field - The ionization field created between the source and the extractor 
electrode.
Faraday – Measuring apparatus, whereby ions or electrons are collected in a small 
metal cup and measured to determine probe current.
Featureless - An area that does not have a pattern. Bare metal, such as that on the 
sample holder, works best.
FIB - Focused Ion Beam
First Light - The point in the Select a Trimer process at which the signal stays there 
for more than a few seconds when slowly increasing the extractor voltage. It is the 
point at which ionization is first observed. It is typically within the range of -20 to -25 kV.
Focus Point - The point where all beam ions converge in a small spot. This is 
controlled by the objective lens (lens 2)
Focus voltage – Voltage applied to lens 2 for the purpose of focusing the beam on the 
sample. It is controlled via a knob on the knob panel.
Focus wobble - Function that sweeps the focus of the objective lens backwards and 
forward through the focus on the sample plane. When the aperture is misaligned a 
lateral shift is observed.
FOV - The size of the area being scanned by the ion beam. In the NanoFab the FOV 
is always a square. For example, setting the image FOV to 1 µm means that the ion 
beam will scan an area of 1 µm by 1 µm, hence the displayed image represents an 
area of 1 µm by 1 µm

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 250](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-250.webp)

## Page 251

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 246 |
Frame Averaging - Each frame is scanned the selected number of times and the 
average signal is displayed.
Ga FIB Column - The Capella Gallium Focused Ion Beam column. an optional 
component of the ORION NanoFab basic system that uses ionized gallium for image 
generation and specimen analysis, milling, and gas assisted etching and deposition.
Gain - The ratio of two quantities. When referring to circuits, it's almost always the ratio 
of some measurement of the output of a circuit or component to its input. The most 
common usage is voltage gain, the ratio between the voltage amplitude of the output 
signal to the input signal, but it could also be power gain or current gain.
Gas Box – The gas manifold through which gas from the He or Ne bottles is 
transported to the inner gun can. It includes the regulators and leak valves and the gas 
purifier.
GFIS - Gas Field Ion Source. The gas field can be either Helium or Neon in the Orion/
Polaris NanoFab.
GIS - Gas Injection System
Image size - The same as Scan Size, the number of pixels in the image.
Kelvin – A unit of measure for temperature. The Kelvin scale is an absolute, 
thermodynamic temperature scale using as its null point absolute zero. Water at 0.0 °C 
= 273.16 K. 100 K = -173.15ºC = -279.67F.
Landing Energy - The energy at which ions hit the sample, determined by the 
accelerator voltage.
Lens 1 – The upper (condenser) lens. When the beam comes through the extractor 
plate on the end of the gun, it is diverging. Lens 1 causes the beam to converge so that 
it focuses at a point after lens 1 and prior to reaching the limiting apertures.
Lens 2 – The lower (objective) lens. Lens 2 focuses the beam on the sample.
Line Averaging: - Each line is scanned the selected number of times and the average 
signal for each pixel is displayed.
NanoFab – The current model of the HIM tool being produced by Zeiss (engineering 
product name is Polaris).
Nanometer – One billionth of a meter (10-9).
Node - In the context of NanoFab circuitry, a node is a replaceable board that is 
connected to an Ethernet circuit, such as a stage board or vacuum board.
Normal imaging mode – The mode in which the ion beam generated by the 
microscope generates an ion beam that is deflected by the octopoles and scanned 
across the sample. The image displayed in the UI is of your sample.
NPVE - NanoPatterning and Visualization Engine – a component that allows you to 
perform intricate and specific patterns in both etching and deposition processes.
Octopoles (Octopole Deflectors) – Mechanism for deflecting the ion beam for the 
purpose of scanning across the target.

5/29/18
Operations Center - A tool panel available in the Zen UI which allows users to view 
and alter component power states.
Outgassing - The release of adsorbed or occluded gases or water vapor, usually by 
heating, in a vacuum.
Patterning - The process of removing sample atoms in specific areas on the sample 
with the ion beam. During patterning the ion beam is vector-scanned across the 
sample surface. The beam stays in a single scan point for a designated time (dwell 
time) before it moves to the next scan point.
Polaris - The Zeiss engineering product name for the current version of the NanoFab.
Power Cycle - To power down and power back up. This typically applies to rack 1 or 
rack 2, which house several sub-system boards.
Power Down - A state in which a hardware device’s power supplies are disabled. A 
powered down component is considered safe, but cannot be used to perform its task 
from its current state. Depending on the component, the time to power up from a 
Powered Down state may be lengthy. Many IO points from a powered down 
component may still be available for readback.
Power Up - A state in which a component can perform its designed purpose.
Preset - A set of system settings that can be applied as a group, and can include 
column settings, aperture selection, detector settings, imaging parameters, etc.
Probe - A set of column settings that configure a column for a specific beam current, 
landing energy, and beam size.
Probe Table - A collection of probes.
Quadrupoles – Mechanism below lens 1 with the main purpose of directing the ion 
beam to be on-axis with the center of lens 2.
Rack 1 - Also known as optics rack. The left hand side of the upper portion of the 
electronics rack. It contains the GFIS1 optics controller, GFIS2 optics support 
controller, SAS controller, and Capella controller if a Capella column is installed.
Rack 2 - Also known as detector rack. The right hand side of the upper portion of the 
electronics rack. It contains the Internet hub, flood gun controller, ET detector 
controller, and the stage controller.
Raster scan pattern - the beam sweeps horizontally left-to-right at a steady rate, then 
blanks and rapidly moves back to the left, where it turns back on and sweeps out the 
next line.
SAS - Scanning and Acquisition system. The SAS controller defines the scanning 
parameters, such image size, dwell time, field of view, etc., and brings in the video 
signal from the ET detector to create the ion beam images.
Scan Size - The number of pixels per line (x-direction) and the number of lines (y-
direction) per frame (one full scan of the field of view).
Scan Speed - The rate at which a sample is scanned. Scan speed is established by 
setting dwell time, which is the amount of time in which the beam remains at a single 
scan point.

![ZEISS ORION NanoFab Operator Manual Rev2 - page 252](../../assets/img/pdf-pages/zeiss-orion-nanofab-operator-manual-rev2/page-252.webp)

*Page 252 facsimile from `ZEISS_ORION_NanoFab_Operator_Manual_Rev2.pdf`.*

## Page 253

5/29/18
360800-0000-024 Rev 2
| 248 |
Scintillator - a disk coated with two very thin and fragile layers of first phosphor and 
then aluminum. It absorbs electrons and fluoresces photons while releasing the 
previously absorbed energy.
Secondary Electron (SE) – Low energy electrons that are liberated from the sample 
surface when the sample is hit by the primary ion or electron beam. They are 
generated by inelastic scattering.
SFIM Mode - Scanning Field Ion Microscope mode – Mode used to for viewing or 
aligning a trimer. In SFIM mode, scanning is done by the quadrupoles, and the image 
displayed in the UI is a representation of the source.
SIMS - Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry - A mass spectrometry technique used to 
analyze the qualitative compositions of solid surfaces and thin films up to a depth of 2 
nm.
Source – The filament which, when appropriately treated and operated, knocks off 
electrons and creates positively charged gas ions.
Spot Control – A gradient scale from one to ten representing an increase of lens 1 
voltage, resulting in a decrease of beam current, lesser column magnification, and 
decreased source vibration, but an increase of resolution. Note that increasing the 
spot control value decreases beam size, and vice versa.
Standby - A state in which a component can be left safely for prolonged periods of 
time and quickly transition back into its Powered Up state.
State Machine - A conceptual model of a machine that can be in a finite number of 
states, transitions, and actions. When the Vacuum Dashboard is in state machine 
mode, the system is controlled by the software, with a limited number of commands 
available to the operator, each of which produce further programmed actions.
Stigmation – The process of removing any astigmatism (non-circular shape) of the 
beam.
Stigmator – Mechanism that compensates for astigmatism (lens aberration), 
contributing to the formation of a rotationally symmetrical beam. The upper octopole 
serves this purpose (as well as beam deflection).
UPS - Uninterruptable Power Supply. Also referred to as PCU – Electrical apparatus 
that provides emergency power to a load when the input power source fails.
Wizard - A set of software routines in the GFIS Source Maintenance, System 
Maintenance, and other tool panels of the Zen UI that perform actions such as 
obtaining and aligning a trimer, or aligning eucentric working distance.
Wobble - A function that sweeps the focus of the objective lens (lens 2) backward and 
forward through the focus on the specimen plane. When the aperture is misaligned a 
lateral shift is observed. Wobble is used in conjunction with tilt & shift for beam 
alignment.
Working Distance (WD) - The distance from the bottom of lens 2 to the sample when 
focused, as calculated by the software.
Zen - The new user interface that contains the controls for operating the NanoFab & 
the screens for displaying images and graphics.
